by some subrepresentation
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\frac{\mathcal{V}_{\Delta}}{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}\ .

(2.1.10)

In other words, a degenerate representation is associated with a nontrivial subrepresentation of a Verma module.
Now, in any nontrivial subrepresentation of a highest-weight representation, the
L_{0}
eigenvalues are bounded from below, and there is therefore a primary state
|\chi\rangle
.
This primary state
|\chi\rangle
is also a descendant – it is then called a
singular vector
or
null vector
of the highest-weight representation.
While a highest-weight representation is by construction always indecomposable, it is irreducible if and only if it has no singular vectors.

Coming back to our Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{\Delta}
, and assuming that it has a singular vector
|\chi\rangle
, we can define the degenerate representation
\mathcal{R}=\frac{\mathcal{V}_{\Delta}}{U(\mathfrak{V}^{+})|\chi\rangle}
, which is a quotient of two Verma modules.
If a Verma module has several singular vectors, then it also has several corresponding subrepresentations, and therefore a number of possible quotients, depending on which subrepresentation is chosen as the denominator representation
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
in Eq. (
2.1.10
).

Looking for singular vectors at low levels

We start at the level
N=1
: is
|\chi\rangle=L_{-1}|\Delta\rangle
a singular vector? The states
L_{n}|\chi\rangle
with
n\geq 2
automatically vanish because they have negative levels, and we are left with computing

\displaystyle L_{1}|\chi\rangle=L_{1}L_{-1}|\Delta\rangle=[L_{1},L_{-1}]|\Delta\rangle=2L_{0}|\Delta\rangle=2\Delta|\Delta\rangle\ .

(2.1.11)

So the Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{\Delta}
has a singular vector at the level
N=1
if and only if
\Delta=0
, and in particular
\mathcal{V}_{0}
is reducible.
Let us now look for singular vectors at the level
N=2
.
The level-
2
descendants of a primary state
|\Delta\rangle
are of the type

\displaystyle|\chi\rangle=\left(a_{1,1}L_{-1}^{2}+a_{2}L_{-2}\right)|\Delta\rangle\ ,

(2.1.12)

where
a_{1,1}
and
a_{2}
are complex coefficients.
We compute

\displaystyle L_{1}|\chi\rangle

\displaystyle=\left((4\Delta+2)a_{1,1}+3a_{2}\right)L_{-1}|\Delta\rangle\ ,

(2.1.13)

\displaystyle L_{2}|\chi\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(6\Delta a_{1,1}+(4\Delta+\tfrac{1}{2}c)a_{2}\right)|\Delta\rangle\ .

(2.1.14)

Then
L_{1}|\chi\rangle=L_{2}|\chi\rangle=0
is a system of two linear equations for the two unknowns
(a_{1,1},a_{2})
, whose determinant is

\displaystyle D_{2}(\Delta)=4(2\Delta+1)^{2}+(c-13)(2\Delta+1)+9\ .

(2.1.15)

Singular vectors at the level
N=2
exist if and only if

\displaystyle D_{2}(\Delta)=0\quad\iff\quad\Delta=\frac{5-c\pm\sqrt{(c-25)(c-1)}}{16}\ .

(2.1.16)

As our last explicit example, we look for a singular vector at the level
N=3
,

\displaystyle|\chi\rangle=\left(a_{1,1,1}L_{-1}^{3}+a_{1,2}L_{-1}L_{-2}+a_{3}L_{-3}\right)|\Delta\rangle\ .

(2.1.17)

The nontrivial relations that
|\chi\rangle
must obey in order to be a singular vector are
L_{1}|\chi\rangle=L_{2}|\chi\rangle=L_{3}|\chi\rangle=0
.
However, since
L_{3}=[L_{2},L_{1}]
, the relation
L_{3}|\chi\rangle=0
is actually redundant, and we need only compute

\displaystyle L_{1}|\chi\rangle

\displaystyle=\Big((6\Delta+6)a_{1,1,1}+3a_{1,2}\Big)L_{-1}^{2}|\Delta\rangle+\Big((2\Delta+4)a_{1,2}+4a_{3}\Big)L_{-2}|\Delta\rangle\ ,

(2.1.18)

\displaystyle L_{2}|\chi\rangle

\displaystyle=\Big((18\Delta+6)a_{1,1,1}+(4\Delta+\tfrac{1}{2}c+9)a_{1,2}+5a_{3}\Big)L_{-1}|\Delta\rangle\ .

(2.1.19)

This leads to a system of three linear equations for the three unknowns
(a_{1,1,1},a_{1,2},a_{3})
, whose determinant is

\displaystyle D_{3}(\Delta)=12\Big(3(\Delta+1)^{2}+(c-13)(\Delta+1)+12\Big)\ .

(2.1.20)

Singular vectors at the level
N=3
exist if and only if

\displaystyle D_{3}(\Delta)=0\quad\iff\quad\Delta=\frac{7-c\pm\sqrt{(c-25)(c-1)}}{6}\ .

(2.1.21)

Alternative notations for
c
and
\Delta

Let us simplify these formulas by introducing alternative notations for
c
and
\Delta
.
We introduce two new notations for the central charge
c
: the
background charge

Q
and the
coupling constant

b
,

\displaystyle\boxed{c=1+6Q^{2}}\quad,\quad\boxed{Q=b+\frac{1}{b}}\ ,

(2.1.22)

or equivalently

\displaystyle b=\sqrt{\frac{c-1}{24}}+\sqrt{\frac{c-25}{24}}\ .

(2.1.23)

Let us indicate the correspondences between certain values of
c,Q
and
b
. We pay particular attention to the values
c=1
and
c=25
, which are the critical points of the map
c(b)
, and play an important role in the properties of Virasoro representations:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{|l|c||c|c|c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr\text{central\ charge}&c&\mathbb{C}&\leq 1&1-6\frac{(p-q)^{2}}{pq}&1&[1,25]&25&\geq 25\\
\hline\cr\text{background\ charge}&Q&\mathbb{C}&i{\mathbb{R}}&i\frac{p-q}{\sqrt{pq}}&0&[0,2]&2&\geq 2\\
\hline\cr\text{coupling\ constant}&b&\mathbb{C}^{*}&i{\mathbb{R}}&i\sqrt{\frac{p}{q}}&i&e^{i\mathbb{R}}&1&{\mathbb{R}^{*}}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

where for each generic value of
c
we choose among two corresponding values
\pm Q
for
Q
and four corresponding values
\pm b^{\pm 1}
for
b
.
The conformal dimension
\Delta
can be written in terms of a
new parameter
P
called the
momentum
, such that

\displaystyle\boxed{\Delta(P)=\frac{Q^{2}}{4}-P^{2}}\ ,

(2.1.27)

which is defined up to the
reflection

\displaystyle P\mapsto-P\ .

(2.1.28)

We will sometimes write
\mathcal{V}_{P}=\mathcal{V}_{-P}
for the Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{\Delta(P)}
with conformal dimension
\Delta(P)
. In the literature, it is often the quantity
\alpha=\frac{Q}{2}+P
or
\alpha=\frac{Q}{2}-P
that is called the momentum, but most formulas are simpler in terms of
P
.

Singular vectors at all levels

In terms of the coupling constant
b
, the singular vectors at the levels
N=1,2,3
, and the corresponding conformal dimensions
\Delta
are

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr N&\langle r,s\rangle&\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}&L_{\langle r,s\rangle}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr 1&\langle 1,1\rangle&0&L_{-1}\\
\hline\cr\hbox{\multirowsetup 2}&\langle 2,1\rangle&-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3}{4}b^{2}&\frac{1}{b^{2}}L_{-1}^{2}+L_{-2}\\
\cline{2-4}\cr&\langle 1,2\rangle&-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3}{4b^{2}}&b^{2}L_{-1}^{2}+L_{-2}\\
\hline\cr\hbox{\multirowsetup 3}&\langle 3,1\rangle&-1-2b^{2}&\frac{1}{4b^{2}}L_{-1}^{3}+L_{-1}L_{-2}+(b^{2}-\frac{1}{2})L_{-3}\\
\cline{2-4}\cr&\langle 1,3\rangle&-1-\frac{2}{b^{2}}&\frac{1}{4}b^{2}L_{-1}^{3}+L_{-1}L_{-2}+(\frac{1}{b^{2}}-\frac{1}{2})L_{-3}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Singular vectors at levels
N\geq 4
can similarly be computed. (See Exercises
2.3
and
2.4
.) The general result is
[
5
]
: for any factorization
N=rs
of
N
into two positive integers, there is a number
\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}
such that the Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}}
has a singular vector at the level
N
, which we denote as

\displaystyle|\chi_{\langle r,s\rangle}\rangle=L_{\langle r,s\rangle}|\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}\rangle\ .

(2.1.35)

The general formula for
\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}
is

\displaystyle\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}=\frac{1}{4}\left(Q^{2}-(rb+sb^{-1})^{2}\right)\ .

(2.1.36)

This corresponds to the momemtums
\pm P_{\langle r,s\rangle}
, where

\displaystyle\boxed{P_{\langle r,s\rangle}=\frac{1}{2}\left(rb+sb^{-1}\right)}\ .

(2.1.37)

(For a simple derivation using fusion rules, see Section
2.3.1
.)
Conversely, any singular vector that is not itself a descendant of another singular vector is of the type
|\chi_{\langle r,s\rangle}\rangle
.
However, singular vectors that are descendants of other singular vectors are not necessarily of this type. (See
Exercise
2.5
.) We define the
maximally degenerate representation

\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s\rangle}
as the quotient of
\mathcal{V}_{\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}}
by the subrepresentation generated by all the singular vectors.
Then
\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s\rangle}
is irreducible.
For generic values of
c
,
|\chi_{\langle r,s\rangle}\rangle
is the only singular vector of
\mathcal{V}_{\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}}
, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s\rangle}=\frac{\mathcal{V}_{\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}}}{U(\mathfrak{V}^{+})|\chi_{\langle r,s\rangle}\rangle}=\frac{\mathcal{V}_{\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}}}{\mathcal{V}_{\Delta_{\langle-r,s\rangle}}}\ ,

(2.1.38)

where
\Delta_{\langle-r,s\rangle}
is actually the conformal dimension of
|\chi_{\langle r,s\rangle}\rangle
, due to the identity

\displaystyle\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}+rs=\Delta_{\langle-r,s\rangle}\ .

(2.1.39)

For particular values of
c
such that
\mathcal{V}_{\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}}
has more than one singular vector, the structure of
\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s\rangle}
is more complicated, see Exercises
2.5
and
2.6
.

2.1.3
Unitarity

Unitarity and singular vectors

Let us discuss which highest-weight representations of the Virasoro algebra are unitary, starting with Verma modules. Let us assume there is a Hermitian form (also called a scalar product) such that
L_{n}^{\dagger}=L_{-n}
Eq. (
1.4.5
).
This conjugation rule allows us to deduce scalar products of descendant states, from the square norm of the primary state
|\Delta\rangle
of a Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{\Delta}
, which we normalize to
1
and write
\langle\Delta|\Delta\rangle=1
.
For example,

\displaystyle\langle L_{-1}\Delta|L_{-1}\Delta\rangle=\langle\Delta|L_{-1}^{\dagger}L_{-1}|\Delta\rangle=\langle\Delta|L_{1}L_{-1}|\Delta\rangle=2\langle\Delta|L_{0}|\Delta\rangle=2\Delta\ .

(2.1.40)

Since
L_{0}
is self-conjugate, different
L_{0}
-eigenspaces are orthogonal, and
\mathcal{V}_{\Delta}
is unitary if and only if the scalar product is positive definite on each eigenspace.
Given a basis
\{v_{i}\}
of the level-
N
eigenspace
\mathcal{R}_{N}
, the scalar product on
\mathcal{R}_{N}
is characterized by the Gram matrix
M^{(N)}
defined by
M^{(N)}_{ij}=\langle v_{i}|v_{j}\rangle
, and in particular the scalar product is positive definite if and only if all the eigenvalues of
M^{(N)}
are strictly positive. This implies in particular
\det M^{(N)}>0
.

It is easy to see that
\det M^{(N)}
must be a polynomial function of
\Delta
and
c
.
In order to determine its sign, we should study its zeros. These zeros are determined by the property:

\det M^{(N)}=0
if and only if there is a singular vector at a level
N^{\prime}\leq N
.

(See Exercise
2.7
.)
We therefore know the zeros
\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}
of
\det M^{(N)}
as a function of
\Delta
Eq. (
2.1.36
), and we obtain the
Kac determinant
formula

\displaystyle\boxed{\det M^{(N)}\propto\prod_{\begin{smallmatrix}r,s\geq 1\\
rs\leq N\end{smallmatrix}}(\Delta-\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle})^{p(N-rs)}}\ ,

(2.1.41)

where the constant of proportionality is a
\Delta,c
-independent positive number, and
p(N^{\prime})
is the number of partitions of
N^{\prime}
, in particular
p(0)=1
.
The multiplicity of the zero
\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}
is
p(N-rs)
because this is the number of linearly independent descendants at the level
N
of a singular vector at the level
rs
.

Unitarity of Verma modules and degenerate representations

According to Eq. (
2.1.40
), the positivity of
M^{(1)}
implies
\Delta>0
.
The unitarity of
\mathcal{V}_{\Delta}
will now depend on the value of the central charge
c
, which must be real
as we already saw in Section
1.4.1
:

1.

If
c>1
, then
\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}
(
2.1.36
) cannot be a real, strictly positive number.
Therefore, eigenvalues of
\det M^{(N)}
do not change signs on the half-line
c>1
.
In the limit
c\to\infty
, the matrix
M^{(N)}
is positive definite. (See Exercise
2.8
.)
Therefore,
\mathcal{V}_{\Delta}
is unitary.

2.

If
c=1
, then the Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{\Delta}
is unitary if
\Delta>0
by continuity from the
c>1
case, unless
\Delta=\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}
for some
\langle r,s\rangle
, that is unless
\Delta=\frac{1}{4}n^{2}
with
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

3.

If
c<1
, then the Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{\Delta}
is never unitary.
This is because for a given
\Delta>0
, the values of
c
such that
\Delta=\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}
for some
\langle r,s\rangle
accumulate at
c=1^{-}
. By tracking sign changes, one can show that
\det M^{(N)}\leq 0
for some
N

[
5
]
.

We still have to consider the unitarity of degenerate highest-weight representations of the type
\mathcal{R}=\frac{\mathcal{V}_{\Delta}}{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}
, where the subrepresentation
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
contains at least one singular vector.
For
\mathcal{R}
to be unitary, the subrepresentation
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
must include all negative-norm states, that is all eigenstates of Gram matrices with negative eigenvalues.

If
c>1
, the only degenererate representation with a positive conformal dimension is
\mathcal{R}_{\langle 1,1\rangle}
, and actually
\Delta_{\langle 1,1\rangle}=0
. This representation is unitary, because all the zero-norm states in
\mathcal{V}_{0}=\underset{\Delta\to 0^{+}}{\lim}\mathcal{V}_{\Delta}
are descendants of the level one singular vector. If
c=1
, all degenerate representations have positive conformal dimensions, and again they are unitary.

If now
c<1
, it turns out that in order to have a unitary quotient, a Verma module must have not only one but actually two independent singular vectors
[
5
]
.
Since the existence of a singular vector in
\mathcal{V}_{\Delta}
implies a relation between
\Delta
and the central charge
c
, the existence of two singular vectors implies a constraint on
c
.
As we will see in Section
3.2.2
, the resulting values of
c
correspond to the minimal models, whose central charges are given in Eq. (
•
‣
1.4.3
).
However, the existence of two singular vectors is a necessary, but not yet a sufficient condition for a Verma module to have a unitary quotient.
The final result is that for
c<1
unitary quotients exist only if the central charge
c
takes values that correspond to the unitary minimal models,

\displaystyle c_{p,p+1}=1-\frac{6}{p(p+1)}\quad\text{with}\quad 2\leq p\ ,

(2.1.42)

For these values of
c
,
the conformal dimensions of Verma modules that have unitary quotients are
\Delta=\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}=\Delta_{\langle p-r,p+1-s\rangle}
where

\displaystyle 1\leq s\leq r\leq p-1\ .

(2.1.43)

These unitary quotients are the maximally degenerate representations
\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s\rangle}
, which take the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s\rangle}=\frac{\mathcal{V}_{\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}}}{U(\mathfrak{V}^{+})|\chi_{\langle r,s\rangle}\rangle+U(\mathfrak{V}^{+})|\chi_{\langle p-r,p+1-s\rangle}\rangle}\ ,

(2.1.44)

where the sum of the two subrepresentations is not a direct sum, because their intersection is nonzero. One way to prove that such representations are unitary is to identify them with representations of quotients of affine Lie algebras, in the context of the description of minimal models as quotients of WZW models
[
5
]
.

These results are summarized in the following table:

central charge

c<1

c=1

c>1

\mathcal{V}_{\Delta}
unitary?

no

\Delta>0
and
\Delta\neq\frac{1}{4}n^{2}

\Delta>0

\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s\rangle}
unitary?

see Eqs. (
2.1.42
) and (
2.1.43
)

yes

\langle r,s\rangle=\langle 1,1\rangle

2.2
Fields and correlation functions

Using the state-field correspondence, the action of the Virasoro algebra on states gives rise to a natural action of the Virasoro algebra on fields at any given point
z
. We denote the action of a Virasoro generator as
L_{n}^{(z)}
or
L_{n}
, so that

\displaystyle L_{n}V_{\sigma}(z)=L_{n}^{(z)}V_{\sigma}(z)=V_{L_{n}\sigma}(z)\ .

(2.2.1)

(By definition,
L_{n}^{(z_{2})}V_{\sigma_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\sigma_{2}}(z_{2})=V_{\sigma_{1}}(z_{1})L_{n}V_{\sigma_{2}}(z_{2})
.)
Let a
primary field
be a field that corresponds to a primary state.
A primary field

V_{\Delta}(z)
of conformal dimension
\Delta
obeys

\displaystyle\boxed{\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}L_{n>0}V_{\Delta}(z)=0\ ,\\
L_{0}V_{\Delta}(z)=\Delta V_{\Delta}(z)\ .\end{array}\right.}

Fields such as
\left(\prod_{i=1}^{p}L_{-n_{i}}\right)V_{\Delta}(z)
that correspond to descendants of a primary state are then called
descendant fields
.

Having two Virasoro symmetry algebras
\mathfrak{V}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{V}}
, we also introduce left and right primary fields
V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(z)
, which are primary with respect to both algebras. Then
\Delta
and
\bar{\Delta}
are respectively called the left and right conformal dimensions of
V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(z)
. A
diagonal primary field

V_{\Delta,\Delta}(z)
is a primary field whose left and right dimensions coincide.

In order to solve a model whose spectrum is known, we should compute the correlation functions of primary and descendant fields.
An
N
-point function of primary fields
\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\Delta_{i},\bar{\Delta}_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle
is a function of their positions and conformal dimensions.

N
-point functions of descendant fields are of the type
\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\left(\left(\prod_{j=1}^{p_{i}}L_{-n_{i,j}}\prod_{\bar{j}=1}^{\bar{p}_{i}}\bar{L}_{-\bar{n}_{i,\bar{j}}}\right)V_{\Delta_{i},\bar{\Delta}_{i}}(z_{i})\right)\right\rangle
, with
p_{i},\bar{p}_{i}\geq 0
and
n_{i,j},\bar{n}_{i,\bar{j}}\geq 1
.

Axiom 2.1

(Single-valuedness of correlation functions)

Correlation functions are single-valued functions, and in particular have trivial monodromies when the fields move around one another.

This axiom is important enough that it deserves an explicit statement, although strictly speaking it follows from our definition of correlation functions.
Relaxing this axiom can be fruitful, in particular by allowing the existence of parafermionic fields.

2.2.1
The energy-momentum tensor

In order to derive equations that constrain the correlation functions, we will now explain how the action of conformal symmetry is encoded in a field, the energy-momentum tensor.

Definition

Axiom 2.2

(Dependence of fields on the position)

For any field
V_{\sigma}(z)
,

\displaystyle\boxed{{\frac{\partial}{\partial z}V_{\sigma}(z)}=L_{-1}V_{\sigma}(z)}\quad\text{and}\quad{\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}}V_{\sigma}(z)}=\bar{L}_{-1}V_{\sigma}(z)\ ,

(2.2.4)

consistently with the interpretation of
L_{-1},\bar{L}_{-1}
as generators of translations.

The action of the left-moving Virasoro algebra will therefore give rise to holomorphic derivatives
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
, and the solutions of the corresponding differential equations will be holomorphic functions of
z
. The analogous results for the right-moving Virasoro algebra are obtained by
z\to\bar{z}
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\to\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}}
.

From Axiom
2.2
we can immediately deduce how
L_{n}^{(z)}
depends on
z
. Applying the axiom to the two fields
V_{\sigma}(z)
and
L_{n}^{(z)}V_{\sigma}(z)
, we find
\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}}L_{n}^{(z)}=0
and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z}L_{n}^{(z)}=[L_{-1},L_{n}^{(z)}]=-(n+1)L_{n-1}^{(z)}\ .

(2.2.5)

(See Exercise
2.9
for the corresponding Witt algebra identity.)
This shows that the Virasoro generators at different points
(L_{n}^{(z_{1})})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
(L_{n}^{(z_{2})})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
are linearly related, and should be understood of two bases of the same space of symmetry generators. Let us now introduce a generating function
T(y)
for
(L_{n}^{(z)})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, such that the equations that determine the
z
-dependence of
L_{n}^{(z)}
become
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}T(y)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}}T(y)=0
. The appropriate generating function is the
energy-momentum tensor

\displaystyle\boxed{T(y)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{L_{n}^{(z)}}{(y-z)^{n+2}}}\ .

(2.2.6)

(The series should converge if
y
is close enough to
z
.) Calling
T(y)
the energy-momentum tensor is a stretch of terminology which is specific to two-dimensional CFT: when specialized to a two-dimensional space with complex coordinates, the general definition of the energy-momentum tensor actually yields the matrix

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}T_{yy}=T(y)&T_{y\bar{y}}\\
T_{\bar{y}y}&T_{\bar{y}\bar{y}}=\bar{T}(y)\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, where in particular
\bar{T}(y)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{\bar{L}_{n}^{(z)}}{(\bar{y}-\bar{z})^{n+2}}
.

Analytic properties

The energy-momentum tensor encodes the action of the Virasoro algebra at all points
z
.
From
T(y)
, we can indeed recover the Virasoro generators at any point,

\displaystyle\boxed{L_{n}^{(z)}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{z}dy(y-z)^{n+1}T(y)}\ .

(2.2.7)

So, for any field
V_{\sigma}(z)
, we have

\displaystyle T(y)V_{\sigma}(z)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{L_{n}V_{\sigma}(z)}{(y-z)^{n+2}}\ .

(2.2.8)

This is actually a special case of the general OPE (
1.2.6
), where the sum over the spectrum of the theory reduces to a combination of fields of the type of
L_{n}V_{\sigma}(z)
, as follows from the definition of
T(y)
as a combination of symmetry generators.
This OPE simplifies if
V_{\sigma}(z)
is a primary field. Using Eqs. (
2.2
) and (
2.2.4
), we have in this case

\displaystyle\boxed{T(y)V_{\Delta}(z)=\frac{\Delta V_{\Delta}(z)}{(y-z)^{2}}+\frac{\partial V_{\Delta}(z)}{y-z}+O(1)}\ ,

(2.2.9)

where
O(1)
is regular in the limit
y\to z
.
In many calculations,
T(y)
is determined by its poles and residues, and the regular term does not contribute.
This is because by definition
T(y)
is holomorphic at any point
y\in\mathbb{C}
where no field is present.
Since our field theory lives on the Riemann sphere,
T(y)
should be holomorphic at
y=\infty
as well. Let us specify what this means. (See Exercise
2.10
for a justification.)

Axiom 2.3

(Holomorphy of the energy-momentum tensor at infinity)

\displaystyle\boxed{T(y)\underset{y\to\infty}{=}O\left(\frac{1}{y^{4}}\right)}\ ,

(2.2.10)

Interpretation

The commutation relations (
1.3.15
) of the Virasoro algebra are equivalent to the following OPE of the field
T(y)
with itself,

\displaystyle\boxed{T(y)T(z)=\frac{\frac{c}{2}}{(y-z)^{4}}+\frac{2T(z)}{(y-z)^{2}}+\frac{\partial T(z)}{y-z}+O(1)}\ .

(2.2.11)

The proof of this equivalence is sketched in Exercise
2.11
.
The term
\frac{c}{2}
should be understood as
\frac{c}{2}I
where
I
is the
central generator of the Virasoro algebra.
We can consider
I
as a field called the

identity field
, whose presence does not affect correlation functions, and which is in particular
z
-independent,

\displaystyle\left\langle I\cdots\right\rangle=\left\langle\cdots\right\rangle\ .

(2.2.12)

The energy-momentum tensor itself can be seen as a descendant of the identity field. (See Exercise
2.12
.)

The energy-momentum tensor is a
symmetry field
: a field that encodes the action of symmetry transformations, but does not necessarily correspond to a state in the spectrum.
That field nevertheless obeys the same axioms of commutativity and existence of an OPE, as the fields
V_{\sigma}(z)
.
More generally, let a
Virasoro field
be a holomorphic field
T(y)
that obeys the OPE (
2.2.11
).
From such a field, we can deduce a Virasoro symmetry algebra via Eq. (
2.2.7
), so
T(y)
is a symmetry field. However, that Virasoro symmetry may be unrelated to conformal symmetry, in which case it does not obey Axiom
2.2
.
Most results in Section
2
will hold for Virasoro fields and non-conformal Virasoro symmetries, including: Ward identities, nontrivial factors of conformal blocks, and fusion rules. We will however work under Axiom
2.2
, as this simplifies some calculations.

Finally, notice that all our formulas are consistent with dimensional analysis, if we adopt the following dimensions:

\displaystyle\boxed{[z]=-1,\quad[T]=2,\quad[L_{n}]=-n,\quad[V_{\Delta}]=\Delta}\ .

(2.2.13)

Here we anticipate that the dimension of
V_{\Delta}
is given by the eigenvalue of
L_{0}
when acting on that primary field.

2.2.2
Ward identities

We now derive linear equations for correlation functions of primary and/or descendant fields
V_{\sigma_{i}}(z_{i})
, called the Virasoro
Ward identities
or conformal Ward identities.
These identities follow from the properties of the energy-momentum tensor
T(z)
, which translate into the following properties of the correlation function
\left\langle T(z)\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\sigma_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle
as a function of
z
:

•

it is holomorphic on
{\mathbb{C}}-\{z_{1},\cdots z_{N}\}
,

•

its behaviour at
z=z_{i}
is controlled by the OPE (
2.2.8
),

•

its behaviour at
z=\infty
is controlled by Axiom
2.3
.

For any meromorphic function
\epsilon(z)
, with no poles outside
\{z_{1},\cdots z_{N}\}
, we therefore have

\displaystyle\oint_{\infty}dz\ \epsilon(z)\left\langle T(z)\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\sigma_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=0\quad\text{provided}\quad\epsilon(z)\underset{z\to\infty}{=}O(z^{2})\ ,

(2.2.14)

where the contour of integration encloses all the points
z_{1},\cdots z_{N}
.
We will distinguish two types of symmetry equations:

•

global Ward identities
, which are obtained if
\epsilon(z)
is holomorphic, thus a polynomial of degree two,

•

local Ward identities
, which are obtained if
\epsilon(z)\underset{z\to\infty}{=}O(\frac{1}{z})
, so that
\epsilon(z)
must have poles.

All symmetry equations can be obtained as linear combinations of such local and global Ward identities.

Local Ward identities

A spanning set of local Ward identities can be obtained by taking
\epsilon(z)=\frac{1}{(z-z_{i})^{n-1}}
with
n\geq 2
and
i=1,\cdots,N
in Eq. (
2.2.14
).
Since the integrand has singularities only at
z_{1},\cdots z_{N}
, we have
\oint_{\infty}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\oint_{z_{i}}
.
Using the

TV_{\sigma}
OPE (
2.2.8
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle\left(L_{-n}^{(z_{i})}+(-1)^{n+1}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{p=-1}^{\infty}\frac{\binom{p+n-1}{p+1}}{(z_{i}-z_{j})^{n+p}}L_{p}^{(z_{j})}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{N}V_{\sigma_{j}}(z_{j})\right\rangle=0\ ,

(2.2.15)

where
\binom{p+n-1}{p+1}
is a binomial coefficient.
The sum over
p
has finitely many nonzero terms, because
L_{p}^{(z_{j})}V_{\sigma_{j}}(z_{j})=0
if
p
exceeds the level
N_{j}
of the descendant state
\sigma_{j}
.
Using Eq. (
2.2.4
), and the fact that
\sigma_{j}
is an
L_{0}
-eigenstate, the terms with
p=-1,0
involve differential operators, whereas the terms with
p\geq 1
involve annihilation operators.
Therefore, the local Ward identity amounts to writing
\left\langle L_{-n}^{(z_{i})}\prod_{j=1}^{N}V_{\sigma_{j}}(z_{j})\right\rangle
, whose total level is
n+\sum_{j}N_{j}
, in terms of correlation functions whose total levels do not exceed
\sum_{j}N_{j}
. By induction on the total level,

the repeated use of local Ward identities yields an expression for any
N
-point function of descendant fields, as a differential operator of the corresponding
N
-point function of primary fields.

This result is a consequence of the OPE
T(y)V_{\Delta}(z)=(\text{differential operator})V_{\Delta}(z)+O(1)
(
2.2.9
). It no longer holds if we enlarge the Virasoro algebra into a W-algebra, or for non-conformal Virasoro symmetry i.e. in the absence of Axiom
2.2
.

If the fields with indices
j\neq i
are primary, the local Ward identity simplifies to

\displaystyle\boxed{\left\langle L_{-n}^{(z_{i})}V_{\sigma_{i}}(z_{i})\prod_{j\neq i}V_{\Delta_{j}}(z_{j})\right\rangle=\sum_{j\neq i}\left(-\frac{1}{z_{ji}^{n-1}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}}+\frac{n-1}{z_{ji}^{n}}\Delta_{j}\right)\left\langle V_{\sigma_{i}}(z_{i})\prod_{j\neq i}V_{\Delta_{j}}(z_{j})\right\rangle}\ ,

(2.2.16)

where we assume
n\geq 1
, and we use the notation
z_{ji}=z_{j}-z_{i}
. (See Exercise
2.13
.) Actually, inserting
T(z)
itself in an
N
-point function of primary fields also amounts to acting with a differential operator. This operator is uniquely determined by its poles and residues, which are given by the
TV_{\Delta}
OPE (
2.2.9
), and we find

\displaystyle\boxed{\left\langle T(z)\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\Delta_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{\Delta_{i}}{(z-z_{i})^{2}}+\frac{1}{z-z_{i}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}}\right)\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\Delta_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle}\ .

(2.2.17)

Global Ward identities

Taking
\epsilon(z)\in\{1,z,z^{2}\}
in Eq. (
2.2.14
), we find

\displaystyle\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{N}L_{-1}^{(z_{i})}\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\sigma_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(2.2.18)

\displaystyle\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(L_{0}^{(z_{i})}+z_{i}L_{-1}^{(z_{i})}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\sigma_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(2.2.19)

\displaystyle\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(L_{1}^{(z_{i})}+2z_{i}L_{0}^{(z_{i})}+z_{i}^{2}L_{-1}^{(z_{i})}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\sigma_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=0\ .

(2.2.20)

2.2.3
Global conformal symmetry

Infinitesimal global conformal transformations

We will now study the consequences of the global Ward identities, under the assumption that the fields
V_{\sigma_{i}}(z_{i})
are primary fields. Since however
L_{n\geq 2}
do not appear in the global Ward identities, the results will also be valid if the fields
V_{\sigma_{i}}(z_{i})
are quasi-primary fields, where we define a
quasi-primary field
or
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-primary field with conformal dimension
\Delta
as a field
V_{\Delta}(z)
such that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}L_{1}V_{\Delta}(z)=0\ ,\\
L_{0}V_{\Delta}(z)=\Delta V_{\Delta}(z)\ .\end{array}\right.

For example, the energy-momentum tensor
T(z)
is a quasi-primary field with conformal dimension two, but not a primary field. (See Exercise (
2.12
).)

Using Axiom
2.2
, the global Ward identities for an
N
-point function
\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\Delta_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle
of quasi-primary fields reduce to a system of three differential equations,

\displaystyle\forall a\in\{0,+,-\}\quad,\quad\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}D_{z_{i}}^{-\Delta_{i}}(t^{a})\right)\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\Delta_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(2.2.23)

where we define the differential operators

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}D_{x}^{j}(t^{-})&=-{\frac{\partial}{\partial x}}\ ,\\
D_{x}^{j}(t^{0})&=x{\frac{\partial}{\partial x}}-j\ ,\\
D_{x}^{j}(t^{+})&=x^{2}{\frac{\partial}{\partial x}}-2jx\ .\end{array}\right.

These operators form a representation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
with the generators
(t^{0},t^{+},t^{-})
and commutation relations

\displaystyle[t^{0},t^{\pm}]=\pm t^{\pm}\quad,\quad[t^{+},t^{-}]=2t^{0}\ .

(2.2.27)

The algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
also has a representation in terms of traceless matrices of size two,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}M(t^{+})&=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\ ,\\
M(t^{0})&=\frac{1}{2}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\ ,\\
M(t^{-})&=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\ .\end{array}\right.

Notice that the differential terms of
D^{j}_{x}(t^{a})
correspond to the generators
(\ell_{-1},\ell_{0},\ell_{1})
(
1.3.12
) of global conformal transformations (up to signs).
This shows that global Ward identities encode the covariance of correlation functions under infinitesimal global conformal transformations.

Finite global conformal transformations

The global Ward identities for
finite global conformal transformations (
1.3.1
) are found by exponentiating the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
matrices
M(t^{a})
into elements of the Lie group
SL_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\Delta_{i},\bar{\Delta}_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}T_{g}V_{\Delta_{i},\bar{\Delta}_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle\ ,

(2.2.31)

where we define the image
T_{g}V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(z)
of a quasi-primary field by

\displaystyle\boxed{T_{g}V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(z)=(cz+d)^{-2\Delta}(\bar{c}\bar{z}+\bar{d})^{-2\bar{\Delta}}V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}\left(\frac{az+b}{cz+d}\right)}\quad\text{with}\quad g=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in SL_{2}({\mathbb{C}})\ .

(2.2.32)

(See Exercise
2.14
for an interpretation in terms of representations of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
.) Writing this equation for a left and right quasi-primary field
V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(z)
instead of a left quasi-primary field
V_{\Delta}(z)
allows us to specify the locally antiholomorphic factor
(\bar{c}\bar{z}+\bar{d})^{-2\bar{\Delta}}
, instead of leaving it undetermined.

We can now determine the behaviour of a quasi-primary field
V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(z)
at
z=\infty
, as this is the same as the behaviour of
T_{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(z)=(-z)^{-2\Delta}(-\bar{z})^{-2\bar{\Delta}}V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(-\frac{1}{z})
.
Since
V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(-\frac{1}{z})
is smooth at
z=\infty
, we obtain

\displaystyle\boxed{V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(z)\underset{z\to\infty}{=}O\left(z^{-2\Delta}\bar{z}^{-2\bar{\Delta}}\right)}\ .

(2.2.33)

This is consistent with the assumed behaviour (
2.2.10
) of the energy-momentum tensor, which is a quasi-primary field of dimension
\Delta=2
.
Moreover, this suggests that we may define a field at
z=\infty
by

\displaystyle V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(\infty)=\underset{z\to\infty}{\lim}z^{2\Delta}\bar{z}^{2\bar{\Delta}}V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(z)\ .

(2.2.34)

The behaviour (
2.2.33
) of
V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(z)
at
z=\infty
holds provided no other field is present at
z=\infty
.
To determine the behaviour of a correlation function
\left\langle V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(z)V_{\sigma}(\infty)\cdots\right\rangle
at
z=\infty
, we would need to know the OPE
V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(z)V_{\sigma}(\infty)
.

One may then wonder whether local Ward identities are infinitesimal expressions of the covariance of correlation functions under local conformal transformations (
1.3.9
).
For any holomorphic function
h(z)
, we can define the image of a primary field by

\displaystyle T_{h}V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(z)=h^{\prime}(z)^{\Delta}\overline{h^{\prime}(z)}^{\bar{\Delta}}V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(h(z))\ ,

(2.2.35)

which generalizes the image of a quasi-primary field under a global conformal transformation (
2.2.32
).
However, this makes sense only in domains where
h^{\prime}(z)\notin\{0,\infty\}
. On such domains, the analog of Eq. (
2.2.31
) holds. We did not encounter such restrictions in the case of infinitesimal local conformal transformations: for
h(z)
close to the identity, we have
h^{\prime}(z)\neq 0
, and a singularity
z_{0}
where
h^{\prime}(z_{0})=\infty
gave rise to contributions of
L_{n\leq-2}
descendants of the field at
z_{0}
.

Solving global Ward identities

Let us discuss the solutions of the three global Ward identities (
2.2.23
) for an
N
-point function of primary fields

\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\Delta_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle\ .

Viewing the derivatives
\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\Delta_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle
as
N
unknowns, the properties of the system depend a lot on the value of
N
:

•

\boxed{N=0}
: The global Ward identities are trivial (
0=0
).
The zero-point function, which may be called the sphere partition function, is actually a number rather than a function, and carries no information beyond the value of the central charge
[
15
]
.

•

\boxed{N=1}
: The global Ward identities amount to
\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{1}}\left\langle V_{\Delta_{1}}(z_{1})\right\rangle=\Delta_{1}\left\langle V_{\Delta_{1}}(z_{1})\right\rangle=0
.
So the one-point function is constant, and this constant vanishes unless our primary field is degenerate with a level one null vector.

•

\boxed{N=2}
: We still have more Ward identities than unknowns, and we can obtain a condition on
\Delta_{i}
by eliminating the derivatives from the Ward identities.
The elegant way to do this is to use
\epsilon(z)=(z-z_{1})(z-z_{2})
in Eq. (
2.2.14
), which leads to

\displaystyle(z_{1}-z_{2})(\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2})\Big\langle V_{\Delta_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\Delta_{2}}(z_{2})\Big\rangle=0\ .

(2.2.36)

Assuming
\left\langle V_{\Delta_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\Delta_{2}}(z_{2})\right\rangle\neq 0
, we must have

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}\ .

(2.2.37)

The remaining Ward identities then amount to

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{1}}\Big\langle V_{\Delta_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\Delta_{2}}(z_{2})\Big\rangle=-\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{2}}\Big\langle V_{\Delta_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\Delta_{2}}(z_{2})\Big\rangle=-\frac{2\Delta_{1}}{z_{1}-z_{2}}\Big\langle V_{\Delta_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\Delta_{2}}(z_{2})\Big\rangle\ .

(2.2.38)

Using the notation
z_{12}=z_{1}-z_{2}
, the solution is

\displaystyle\Big\langle V_{\Delta_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\Delta_{2}}(z_{2})\Big\rangle\propto z_{12}^{-2\Delta_{1}}\ ,

(2.2.39)

where the proportionality factor is an arbitrary antiholomorphic function. We determine this function by assuming that our fields are not only left primaries but also right primaries, and find

\displaystyle\boxed{\Big\langle V_{\Delta_{1},\bar{\Delta}_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\Delta_{2},\bar{\Delta}_{2}}(z_{2})\Big\rangle=B_{1}\delta_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}}\delta_{\bar{\Delta}_{1},\bar{\Delta}_{2}}z_{12}^{-2\Delta_{1}}\bar{z}_{12}^{-2\bar{\Delta}_{1}}}\ .

(2.2.40)

Here the symbol
\delta_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}}
is a Kronecker delta or a Dirac delta function, depending on whether the conformal dimensions take discrete or continuous values. We have assumed that the fields have no multiplicities, i.e. two different primary fields cannot have the same dimensions. And we have introduced the
two-point structure constant

B_{1}
, which depends on
(\Delta_{1},\bar{\Delta}_{1})
, or equivalently on
(\Delta_{2},\bar{\Delta}_{2})
. We could perform a field renormalization such that
B_{i}=1
, but this would be incompatible with the analyticity of the three-point structure constant in Liouville theory, as we will see in Section
3.1.2
.

•

\boxed{N=3}
: The three global Ward identities determine the dependence of three-point functions on the three coordinates
z_{i}
, without constraining the conformal dimensions
\Delta_{i}
. Using
\epsilon(z)=(z-z_{2})(z-z_{3})
in Eq. (
2.2.14
), we indeed obtain

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{1}}+\frac{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{3}}{z_{1}-z_{2}}+\frac{\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}}{z_{1}-z_{3}}\right)\Big\langle V_{\Delta_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\Delta_{2}}(z_{2})V_{\Delta_{3}}(z_{3})\Big\rangle=0\ .

(2.2.41)

A solution of this equation, and of the analogous equations for the dependences on
z_{2}
and
z_{3}
, is

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{F}^{(3)}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}|z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=z_{12}^{\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}}z_{23}^{\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{3}}z_{31}^{\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{1}}}\ .

(2.2.42)

(See Exercise
2.15
for the generalization to logarithmic conformal field theory.)
A three-point function of left and right primary fields is therefore of the type

\displaystyle\boxed{\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{3}V_{\Delta_{i},\bar{\Delta}_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=C_{123}\left|\mathcal{F}^{(3)}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}|z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\right|^{2}}\ ,

(2.2.43)

where the
z_{i}
-independent factor

C_{123}
is called a
three-point structure constant
, and the modulus square notation means

\displaystyle|f(\Delta,z)|^{2}=f(\Delta,z)f(\bar{\Delta},\bar{z})\ .

(2.2.44)

•

\boxed{N\geq 4}
: The three differential equations are not enough for controlling the dependence on the
N
variables
z_{i}
, and their general solution is

\displaystyle\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\Delta_{i},\bar{\Delta}_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=\left|\prod_{i<j}z_{ij}^{\delta_{ij}}\right|^{2}F(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots x_{N-3})\ ,

(2.2.45)

where the exponents
\delta_{ij}
are numbers such that

\displaystyle\sum_{j<i}\delta_{ji}+\sum_{i<j}\delta_{ij}=-2\Delta_{i}\ ,

(2.2.46)

and
F(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots x_{N-3})
is an arbitrary function of the
cross-ratios

\displaystyle x_{i}=\frac{(z_{i}-z_{N-2})(z_{N-1}-z_{N})}{(z_{i}-z_{N-1})(z_{N-2}-z_{N})}\ ,

(2.2.47)

which are invariant under the global conformal transformations (
1.3.1
).

Then
F(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots x_{N-3})
is related to an
N
-point function with
(z_{N-2},z_{N-1},z_{N})=(0,\infty,1)
, as we now illustrate in the case
N=4
.

•

\boxed{N=4}
: In this case there is only one cross-ratio

\displaystyle x=\frac{z_{12}z_{34}}{z_{13}z_{24}}\ ,

(2.2.48)

and the general solution is

\displaystyle\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{4}V_{\Delta_{i},\bar{\Delta}_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=\Big|z_{13}^{-2\Delta_{1}}z_{23}^{\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4}}z_{34}^{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{4}}z_{24}^{-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{4}}\Big|^{2}F(x)\ ,

(2.2.49)

for an arbitrary function
F(x)
.
The exponents in the prefactor are a particular solution of Eq. (
2.2.46
), chosen so that

\displaystyle F(x)=\Big\langle V_{\Delta_{1},\bar{\Delta}_{1}}(x)V_{\Delta_{2},\bar{\Delta}_{2}}(0)V_{\Delta_{3},\bar{\Delta}_{3}}(\infty)V_{\Delta_{4},\bar{\Delta}_{4}}(1)\Big\rangle\ .

(2.2.50)

Choosing another solution of the
4
equations for
6
unknowns
(\delta_{ij})_{i<j}
would amount to replacing the function
F(x)
with
x^{\lambda}(1-x)^{\mu}F(x)
for some numbers
\lambda,\mu
. For the behaviour of
F(x)
under field permutations, see Exercise
2.16
.

Conformal spins and single-valuedness

Let us define the
conformal spin
of the field
V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(z,\bar{z})
to be the number
S=\Delta-\bar{\Delta}
. This number determines how that field behaves under rotations,

\displaystyle T_{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}e^{i\theta}&0\\
0&e^{-i\theta}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(z)=e^{2i\theta(\Delta-\bar{\Delta})}V_{\Delta,\bar{\Delta}}(e^{2i\theta}z)\ .

(2.2.51)

A primary field is diagonal if and only if its conformal spin is zero.

In order for the three-point function (
2.2.43
) to be single-valued as required by Axiom
2.1
, we need
S_{1}\pm S_{2}\pm S_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}
, equivalently

\displaystyle\boxed{S_{i}\in\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{Z}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad S_{1}+S_{2}+S_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}}\ .

(2.2.52)

Primary fields with spins
S\in\frac{1}{2}+\mathbb{Z}
are called fermionic. Allowing fermionic fields would require us to modify our commutativity Axiom
1.2
, as fermions anticommute. Considering indeed a two-point function (
2.2.40
) of a fermionic field with itself, we have
\left<V(z_{1})V(z_{2})\right>=-\left<V(z_{2})V(z_{1})\right>
, which suggests
\{V(z_{1}),V(z_{2})\}=0
. For an exposition of fermionic CFTs, including fermionic extensions of minimal models, see
[
16
]
.
It is actually possible to relax the single-valuedness axiom, and have fields with spins
S\notin\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
, such as parafermionic fields. But we will only consider fields with integer spins
S\in\mathbb{Z}
.

The conformal spin then controls how three-point structure constants behave under permutations. For
\sigma
a permutation of
(1,2,3)
, we indeed have

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{F}^{(3)}(\Delta_{\sigma(1)},\Delta_{\sigma(2)},\Delta_{\sigma(3)}|z_{\sigma(1)},z_{\sigma(2)},z_{\sigma(3)})\right|^{2}=\operatorname{sign}(\sigma)^{S_{1}+S_{2}+S_{3}}\left|\mathcal{F}^{(3)}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}|z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\right|^{2}.

(2.2.53)

Since the three-point function is invariant under permutations of the fields, the structure constant must therefore behave as

\displaystyle\boxed{C_{\sigma(1)\sigma(2)\sigma(3)}=\operatorname{sign}(\sigma)^{S_{1}+S_{2}+S_{3}}C_{123}}\ .

(2.2.54)

For example, if fields
1
and
2
are identical, then
C_{123}=C_{213}
, and the three-point function can only be nonzero if the third field has even spin
S_{3}\in 2\mathbb{Z}
.

2.2.4
Operator product expansions

Let us study how local conformal symmetry constrains OPEs. We start with a generic OPE of the type
V_{\sigma_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\sigma_{2}}(z_{2})=\sum_{\sigma_{3}}C_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}^{\sigma_{3}}(z_{1},z_{2})V_{\sigma_{3}}(z_{2})
. Let us insert
\oint_{C}dz(z-z_{2})^{n+1}T(z)
on both sides of the OPE, where the contour
C
encircles both
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
. Assuming
n\geq-1
, and using the
TV
OPE (
2.2.8
), this yields the
OPE Ward identity

\displaystyle\left(L_{n}^{(z_{2})}+\sum_{m=-1}^{n}\binom{m+1}{n+1}z_{12}^{n-m}L_{m}^{(z_{1})}\right)V_{\sigma_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\sigma_{2}}(z_{2})=\sum_{\sigma_{3}}C_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}^{\sigma_{3}}(z_{1},z_{2})L_{n}V_{\sigma_{3}}(z_{2})\ .

(2.2.55)

Global Ward identities
n\in\{-1,0\}

Using Axiom
2.2
, the
n=-1
identity becomes

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{1}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{2}}\right)V_{\sigma_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\sigma_{2}}(z_{2})=\sum_{\sigma_{3}}C_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}^{\sigma_{3}}(z_{1},z_{2})\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{2}}V_{\sigma_{3}}(z_{2})\ .

(2.2.56)

Using the OPE again on the left-hand side, this implies
\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{1}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{2}}\right)C_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}^{\sigma_{3}}(z_{1},z_{2})=0
– translation invariance of the OPE coefficients.
Assuming the fields
V_{\sigma_{i}}
are
L_{0},\bar{L}_{0}
-eigenvectors with the dimensions
(\Delta_{\sigma_{i}},\bar{\Delta}_{\sigma_{i}})
, the
n=0
identity becomes

\displaystyle\left(z_{12}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{1}}+\Delta_{\sigma_{1}}+\Delta_{\sigma_{2}}\right)V_{\sigma_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\sigma_{2}}(z_{2})=\sum_{\sigma_{3}}C_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}^{\sigma_{3}}(z_{1},z_{2})\Delta_{\sigma_{3}}V_{\sigma_{3}}(z_{2})\ .

(2.2.57)

Using the OPE again on the left-hand side, this
determines how OPE coefficients behave under dilations.
Therefore, the two global Ward identities determine the dependence of OPE coefficients on
z_{1},z_{2}
,

\displaystyle C_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}^{\sigma_{3}}(z_{1},z_{2})=C_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}^{\sigma_{3}}\left|z_{12}^{\Delta_{{\sigma_{3}}}-\Delta_{\sigma_{1}}-\Delta_{\sigma_{2}}}\right|^{2}\ ,

(2.2.58)

in agreement with dimensional analysis Eq. (
2.2.13
).

Local Ward identities
n\geq 1

For simplicity, let us specialize to an OPE of two primary fields
V_{\Delta_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\Delta_{2}}(z_{2})
.
The left-hand side of the OPE Ward identity reduces to the terms with
L_{-1}^{(z_{1})}
and
L_{0}^{(z_{1})}
, and therefore to
\left(z_{12}^{n+1}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{1}}+(n+1)\Delta_{1}z_{12}^{n}\right)V_{\Delta_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\Delta_{2}}(z_{2})
.
Let us generically write the OPE as
V_{\Delta_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\Delta_{2}}(z_{2})=z_{12}^{-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}(z_{1},z_{2})
, where
\mathcal{O}(z_{1},z_{2})
is a linear combination of fields at
z_{2}
, with coefficients that depend on
z_{12}
. In this notation, the
n=0
global Ward identity is

\displaystyle\left(z_{12}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{1}}-L_{0}^{(z_{2})}\right)\mathcal{O}(z_{1},z_{2})=0\ .

(2.2.59)

Using this identity for eliminating
z_{1}
-derivatives in the
n\geq 1
identity, we obtain

\displaystyle\Bigg\{L_{n}^{(z_{2})}-z_{12}^{n}\left(n\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+L_{0}^{(z_{2})}\right)\Bigg\}\mathcal{O}(z_{1},z_{2})=0\ .

(2.2.60)

Let us specialize to the case where
\mathcal{O}(z_{1},z_{2})
is a combination of primary fields and their descendants, and focus on the contribution of one primary field with dimension
\Delta_{3}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3}}(z_{1},z_{2})=z_{12}^{\Delta_{3}}\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}z_{12}^{|L|}C^{L|\Delta_{3}\rangle}_{12}LV_{\Delta_{3}}(z_{2})\ ,

(2.2.61)

where
\mathcal{L}
is a basis of the space of creation operators
U(\mathfrak{V}^{+})
.
In this case, for any level
N\geq n
, we extract the coefficient of
z_{12}^{\Delta_{3}+N}
in Eq. (
2.2.60
), and we obtain the local Ward identity

\displaystyle\sum_{|L|=N-n}C^{L|\Delta_{3}\rangle}_{12}(\Delta_{3}+N-n+n\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2})LV_{\Delta_{3}}(z_{2})=\sum_{|L|=N}C^{L|\Delta_{3}\rangle}_{12}L_{n}LV_{\Delta_{3}}(z_{2})\ .

(2.2.62)

This system of linear equations determines the coefficients
C^{L|\Delta_{3}\rangle}_{12}
of descendant fields in terms of the coefficient
C^{|\Delta_{3}\rangle}_{12}=C^{3}_{12}
of the primary field,

\displaystyle C^{L|\Delta_{3}\rangle}_{12}=C^{3}_{12}f^{\Delta_{3},L}_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}}\ ,

(2.2.63)

where
f^{\Delta_{3},L}_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}}
are universal coefficients such that
f^{\Delta_{3},1}_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}}=1
.
Let us show this for
|L|\leq 2
. The cases
(N,n)=(1,1),(2,2),(2,1)
of (
2.2.62
) respectively yield

\displaystyle\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}

\displaystyle=2\Delta_{3}f^{L_{-1}}\ ,

(2.2.64)

\displaystyle\Delta_{3}+2\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}

\displaystyle=6\Delta_{3}f^{L_{-1}^{2}}+(4\Delta_{3}+\tfrac{c}{2})f^{L_{-2}}\ ,

(2.2.65)

\displaystyle(\Delta_{3}+1+\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2})f^{L_{-1}}

\displaystyle=2(2\Delta_{3}+1)f^{L_{-1}^{2}}+3f^{L_{-2}}\ ,

(2.2.66)

where we temporarily use the notation
f^{L}=f^{\Delta_{3},L}_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}}
.
The first equation determines
f^{L_{-1}}
, unless
\Delta_{3}=0
. The next two equations determine

f^{L_{-1}^{2}}
and
f^{L_{-2}}
, unless
\Delta_{3}\in\{\Delta_{\langle 1,2\rangle},\Delta_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\}
.
More generally, given a value of the level
N\geq 2
, the equations with
n=1,2
determine
\{f^{L}\}_{|L|=N}
, unless
\Delta_{3}\in\{\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}\}_{rs=N}
. (See Exercise
2.17
.) If
\Delta_{3}\in\{\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}\}_{r,s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
, then for generic values of
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
there is no solution for
\{f^{L}\}_{L\in\mathcal{L}}
, which means that
V_{\Delta_{3}}
cannot appear in the OPE
V_{\Delta_{1}}V_{\Delta_{2}}
.

Structure of the OPE

Let us write the OPE of two left and right primary fields, while neglecting the contributions of descendant fields:

\displaystyle V_{\Delta_{1},\bar{\Delta}_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\Delta_{2},\bar{\Delta}_{2}}(z_{2})=\sum_{\Delta_{3},\bar{\Delta}_{3}}C^{3}_{12}\left|z_{12}^{\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}}\right|^{2}\Big(V_{\Delta_{3},\bar{\Delta}_{3}}(z_{2})+O(z_{12})\Big)\ .

(2.2.67)

Inserting this in a three-point function, and using the two-point function (
2.2.40
), this leads to

\displaystyle\left<\prod_{i=1}^{3}V_{\Delta_{i},\bar{\Delta}_{i}}(z_{i})\right>=B_{3}C^{3}_{12}\left|z_{12}^{\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}}\Big(z_{23}^{-2\Delta_{3}}+O(z_{12})\Big)\right|^{2}\ .

(2.2.68)

Comparing this with the expression (
2.2.43
) for the three-point function, we find the expression of
C^{3}_{12}
in terms of the two- and three-point structure constants,

\displaystyle C^{3}_{12}=(-1)^{S_{1}-S_{2}+S_{3}}\frac{C_{123}}{B_{3}}\ .

(2.2.69)

Here we wrote the sign prefactor under the single-valuedness assumption (
2.2.52
). Under our further assumption that the spins
S_{i}
are integer, the prefactor can be written as
(-1)^{\sum S_{i}}
.
Let us now write the complete expression of the OPE of two primary fields:

\displaystyle\boxed{V_{\Delta_{1},\bar{\Delta}_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\Delta_{2},\bar{\Delta}_{2}}(z_{2})=\sum_{\Delta_{3},\bar{\Delta}_{3}}(-1)^{\sum S_{i}}\frac{C_{123}}{B_{3}}\left|z_{12}^{\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}}\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}z_{12}^{L}f^{\Delta_{3},L}_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}}L\right|^{2}V_{\Delta_{3},\bar{\Delta}_{3}}(z_{2})}\ ,

(2.2.70)

where the modulus square notation (
2.2.44
) means a product of left-moving
z,\Delta,L
and right-moving
\bar{z},\bar{\Delta},\bar{L}
quantities.

Since OPE Ward identities uniquely determine the contributions of the descendants of a given primary field, fusion multiplicities are trivial in fusion products of highest-weight representations of the Virasoro algebra. By definition, the multiplicity of the Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{\Delta_{3}}
in the fusion product
\mathcal{V}_{\Delta_{1}}\times\mathcal{V}_{\Delta_{2}}
indeed coincides with the dimension of the space of solutions of the OPE Ward identities, and that dimension is one or zero.

The case
z_{2}=\infty
can be obtained from the case
z_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
by a global conformal transformation, and we find

\displaystyle V_{\Delta_{1},\bar{\Delta}_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\Delta_{2},\bar{\Delta}_{2}}(\infty)=\sum_{\Delta_{3},\bar{\Delta}_{3}}(-1)^{\sum S_{i}}\frac{C_{123}}{B_{3}}\left|z_{1}^{\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{3}}\right|^{2}\Big(V_{\Delta_{3},\bar{\Delta}_{3}}(\infty)+O\left(z_{1}^{-1}\right)\Big)\ .

(2.2.71)

In the special case where there is no field at infinity, so that
\Delta_{2}=0
and
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{3}
, this is consistent with the behaviour (
2.2.33
) of
V_{\Delta_{1},\bar{\Delta}_{1}}(z_{1})
near
z_{1}=\infty
.

2.3
Degenerate fields

A primary field that has a vanishing descendant is called a
degenerate field
.
In other words, a degenerate field corresponds to the highest-weight state of a degenerate representation.
The degenerate field that corresponds to the highest-weight state of the degenerate representation
\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s\rangle}
will be denoted
V_{\langle r,s\rangle}(z)
, and obeys the equation

\displaystyle L_{\langle r,s\rangle}V_{\langle r,s\rangle}(z)=0\ ,

(2.3.1)

where
L_{\langle r,s\rangle}
is defined in Eq. (
2.1.35
).
This field differs from the field
V_{\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}}(z)
, which corresponds to the highest-weight state of the Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle}}
, and obeys no such equation.

Inserting Eq. (
2.3.1
) into correlation functions, we obtain linear equations for correlation functions that involve the degenerate field
V_{\langle r,s\rangle}
. Such linear equations are called
null vector equations
.

2.3.1
Fusion rules

Let us focus on null vector equations for three-point functions.
We will show that the null vector equation
\left<L_{\langle r,s\rangle}V_{\langle r,s\rangle}V_{\Delta_{2}}V_{\Delta_{3}}\right>=0
leads to
a constraint on
\Delta_{2}
and
\Delta_{3}
. Using the relation between OPEs and three-point functions, this can be interpreted as the fusion rule of the degenerate representation
\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s\rangle}
.

Three-point functions of descendant fields

Conformal symmetry allows us to write three-point functions of descendant fields as three-point functions of primary fields, times the universal factors

\displaystyle g^{L}_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}}=\frac{\left<LV_{\Delta_{1}}(0)V_{\Delta_{2}}(\infty)V_{\Delta_{3}}(1)\right>}{\left<V_{\Delta_{1}}(0)V_{\Delta_{2}}(\infty)V_{\Delta_{3}}(1)\right>}\ .

(2.3.2)

In the case of
L_{-1}^{n}
descendants, this only involves taking derivatives of three-point conformal blocks,

\displaystyle g^{L_{-1}^{n}}_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}}

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial z_{1}^{n}}\mathcal{F}^{(3)}(\Delta_{i}|z_{i})}{\mathcal{F}^{(3)}(\Delta_{i}|z_{i})}\right|_{(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=(0,\infty,1)}=(\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3})_{n}\ ,

(2.3.3)

where we use the notation

\displaystyle(x)_{n}=\frac{\Gamma(x+n)}{\Gamma(x)}=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(x+i)\ .

(2.3.4)

For more general descendants, we use local Ward identities (
2.2.16
). For example,

\displaystyle g^{L_{-2}}_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}}=\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+2\Delta_{3}\ .

(2.3.5)

Let us rewrite the null vector equations for three-point functions as

\displaystyle\left<V_{\langle r,s\rangle}V_{\Delta_{2}}V_{\Delta_{3}}\right>\neq 0\quad\implies\quad g^{L_{\langle r,s\rangle}}_{\Delta_{\langle r,s\rangle},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}}=0\ ,

(2.3.6)

and proceed to solve this equation in the three simplest cases.

Basic degenerate representations
\mathcal{R}_{\langle 1,1\rangle},\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle},\mathcal{R}_{\langle 1,2\rangle}

We begin with the case
\langle r,s\rangle=\langle 1,1\rangle
.
Using Eq. (
2.1.2
) for
\Delta_{\langle 1,1\rangle}
and
L_{\langle 1,1\rangle}
, we obtain

g^{L_{-1}}_{0,\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}}=0
.
Using Eq. (
2.3.3
) for
g^{L_{-1}}
, this implies

\Delta_{2}=\Delta_{3}
,
which is equivalent to the fusion rule

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{R}_{\langle 1,1\rangle}\times\mathcal{V}_{\Delta}=\mathcal{V}_{\Delta}}\ .

(2.3.7)

In fact,
V_{\langle 1,1\rangle}
is an identity field, see Exercise
2.18
.

Next, let us consider the case
\langle r,s\rangle=\langle 2,1\rangle
.
Using Eq. (
2.1.2
), we get

g^{L_{-2}+\frac{1}{b^{2}}L_{-1}^{2}}_{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3}{4}b^{2},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}}=0
.
Using Eqs. (
2.3.3
) and (
2.3.5
) for
g^{L_{-1}^{2}}
and
g^{L_{-2}}
, this implies

\displaystyle 2(\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{3})^{2}+b^{2}(\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3})-\frac{1}{2}-b^{2}-\frac{3}{8}b^{4}=0\ .

(2.3.8)

In terms of the momentums
P_{2},P_{3}
(
2.1.27
) that correspond to the conformal dimensions
\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}
, this equation becomes

\displaystyle\prod_{\pm,\pm}\left(P_{2}\pm P_{3}\pm\frac{b}{2}\right)=0\ .

(2.3.9)

Up to reflections of
P_{2}
or
P_{3}
, we must therefore have

P_{3}=P_{2}\pm\frac{b}{2}
.
We would similarly find
P_{3}=P_{2}\pm\frac{1}{2b}
if we considered the case
\langle r,s\rangle=\langle 1,2\rangle
.
The resulting fusion rules are

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\times\mathcal{V}_{P}=\sum_{\pm}\mathcal{V}_{P\pm\frac{b}{2}}}\quad,\quad\boxed{\mathcal{R}_{\langle 1,2\rangle}\times\mathcal{V}_{P}=\sum_{\pm}\mathcal{V}_{P\pm\frac{1}{2b}}}\ .

(2.3.10)

These fusion rules could be derived by directly analyzing OPEs, instead of three-point functions. (See Exercise
2.19
.)

Higher degenerate representations
\{\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s\rangle}\}_{rs>2}

The higher degenerate representations’ existence, momentums and fusion rules are in principle dictated by their null vectors. We know how to write these null vectors explicitly if
r=1
or
s=1
, but not in general
[
5
]
.
Instead, we will deduce all we need from the basic degenerate representations and their fusion rules, thanks to the associativity of the fusion product.
This amounts to working with the category of representations of the Virasoro algebra, rather than with the structure of the representations.

The basic idea is to build
\mathcal{R}_{\langle 3,1\rangle}
as the combination
\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}-\mathcal{R}_{\langle 1,1\rangle}
.
From the product
\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\times\mathcal{V}_{P}
, we first deduce

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\right)\times\mathcal{V}_{P}=\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\times\left(\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\times\mathcal{V}_{P}\right)=\mathcal{V}_{P-b}+2\cdot\mathcal{V}_{P}+\mathcal{V}_{P+b}\ .

(2.3.11)

Since we obtain a sum of finitely many Verma modules,
\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}
must be a sum of finitely many degenerate representations. But we know that
\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}
is a sum of two representations with the momentums
\{P_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\pm\frac{b}{2}\}=\{P_{\langle 1,1\rangle},P_{\langle 3,1\rangle}\}
, where
P_{\langle r,s\rangle}
is defined in Eq. (
2.1.37
).
We deduce that these two representations are degenerate. In particular, if we did not know it already, we could deduce that there exists a degenerate representation
\mathcal{R}_{\langle 3,1\rangle}=\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}-\mathcal{R}_{\langle 1,1\rangle}
with the momentum
P_{\langle 3,1\rangle}
and the fusion rule

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\langle 3,1\rangle}\times\mathcal{V}_{P}=\left(\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\right)\times\mathcal{V}_{P}-\mathcal{R}_{\langle 1,1\rangle}\times\mathcal{V}_{P}=\mathcal{V}_{P-b}+\mathcal{V}_{P}+\mathcal{V}_{P+b}\ .

(2.3.12)

Iterating this procedure, let us construct
\{\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s\rangle}\}_{rs>2}
and their fusion rules by recursion on
r,s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.
We first find the fusion rule

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s\rangle}=\mathcal{R}_{\langle r-1,s\rangle}+\mathcal{R}_{\langle r+1,s\rangle}\ ,\quad(r\geq 1)\ ,

(2.3.13)

which allows us to deduce
\mathcal{R}_{\langle r+1,s\rangle}
from
\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle},\mathcal{R}_{\langle r-1,s\rangle}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s\rangle}
. Similarly, the recursion on the second index
s
relies on the fusion rule

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\langle 1,2\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s\rangle}=\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s-1\rangle}+\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s+1\rangle}\ ,\quad(s\geq 1)\ .

(2.3.14)

Using the associativity of the fusion products
\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s\rangle}\times\mathcal{V}_{P}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\langle 1,2\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s\rangle}\times\mathcal{V}_{P}
, the recursion yields

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{R}_{\langle r,s\rangle}\times\mathcal{V}_{P}=\sum_{i=-\frac{r-1}{2}}^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\sum_{j=-\frac{s-1}{2}}^{\frac{s-1}{2}}\mathcal{V}_{P+ib+jb^{-1}}}\ ,

(2.3.15)

where the summation index
i
takes half-integer values if
r
is even, and integer values if
r
is odd.
This fusion product is therefore a sum of

rs
terms.

Fusion product of two degenerate representations

In order to determine
\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{1},s_{1}\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{2},s_{2}\rangle}
, we could use a recursion on
r_{1},s_{1}
using the associativity of
\mathcal{R}_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{1},s_{1}\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{2},s_{2}\rangle}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\langle 1,2\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{1},s_{1}\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{2},s_{2}\rangle}
.
We will however employ a more direct method.
Using the fusion rule (
2.3.15
), we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{1},s_{1}\rangle}\times\mathcal{V}_{P_{\langle r_{2},s_{2}\rangle}}=\sum_{r_{3}\overset{2}{=}r_{2}-r_{1}+1}^{r_{1}+r_{2}-1}\ \sum_{s_{3}\overset{2}{=}s_{2}-s_{1}+1}^{s_{1}+s_{2}-1}\mathcal{V}_{P_{\langle r_{3},s_{3}\rangle}}\ ,

(2.3.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{P_{\langle r_{1},s_{1}\rangle}}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{2},s_{2}\rangle}=\sum_{r_{3}\overset{2}{=}r_{1}-r_{2}+1}^{r_{1}+r_{2}-1}\ \sum_{s_{3}\overset{2}{=}s_{1}-s_{2}+1}^{s_{1}+s_{2}-1}\mathcal{V}_{P_{\langle r_{3},s_{3}\rangle}}\ ,

(2.3.17)

where a superscript
\overset{2}{=}
indicates that the corresponding sum runs by increments of
2
.
These two fusion products encode the vanishing of the null vectors of
\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{1},s_{1}\rangle}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{2},s_{2}\rangle}
respectively.
By definition of fusion rules, the product
\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{1},s_{1}\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{2},s_{2}\rangle}
is constrained by the vanishing of the null vectors of both degenerate representations.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{1},s_{1}\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{2},s_{2}\rangle}\ \overset{\text{formal}}{=}\ \left(\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{1},s_{1}\rangle}\times\mathcal{V}_{P_{\langle r_{2},s_{2}\rangle}}\right)\bigcap\left(\mathcal{V}_{P_{\langle r_{1},s_{1}\rangle}}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{2},s_{2}\rangle}\right)\ .

(2.3.18)

This relation is formal in the sense that it determines the momentums of the representations that appear in
\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{1},s_{1}\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{2},s_{2}\rangle}
, but does not say whether these representations are Verma modules or degenerate representations.
However, we do know that these representations must all be degenerate, because
\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{1},s_{1}\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{2},s_{2}\rangle}\times\mathcal{V}_{P}
is a finite sum of Verma modules by associativity.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{1},s_{1}\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{2},s_{2}\rangle}=\sum_{r_{3}\overset{2}{=}|r_{1}-r_{2}|+1}^{r_{1}+r_{2}-1}\ \sum_{s_{3}\overset{2}{=}|s_{1}-s_{2}|+1}^{s_{1}+s_{2}-1}\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{3},s_{3}\rangle}}\ .

(2.3.19)

So the fusion product
\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{1},s_{1}\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{2},s_{2}\rangle}
is a sum of
\min(r_{1},r_{2})\times\min(s_{1},s_{2})
degenerate representations.
This can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{1},s_{1}\rangle}\times\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{2},s_{2}\rangle}=\sum_{r_{3},s_{3}=1}^{\infty}f_{r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}}f_{s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}}\mathcal{R}_{\langle r_{3},s_{3}\rangle}\ ,

(2.3.20)

with the coefficients

\displaystyle f_{r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}1\quad\text{if}\quad\forall i\neq j\neq k\ ,\quad r_{i}+r_{j}-r_{k}\in 1+2{\mathbb{N}}\ .\\
0\quad\text{otherwise}\ .\end{array}\right.

2.3.2
BPZ differential equations

Using the representation (
2.2.16
) of creation operators as differential operators, null vector equations for correlation functions of primary fields become differential equations, called
BPZ equations
after Belavin, Polyakov and Zamolodchikov.
For example, in the cases of the degenerate fields
V_{\langle 1,1\rangle}(x)
and
V_{\langle 2,1\rangle}(x)
, Eq. (
2.3.1
) gives rise to the following BPZ equations:

\displaystyle{\frac{\partial}{\partial x}}\left\langle V_{\langle 1,1\rangle}(x)\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}V_{\Delta_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=0\ ,

(2.3.23)

\displaystyle\boxed{\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}+b^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}\left[\frac{1}{x-z_{j}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}}+\frac{\Delta_{j}}{(x-z_{j})^{2}}\right]\right)\left\langle V_{\langle 2,1\rangle}(x)\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}V_{\Delta_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=0}\ .

(2.3.24)

More generally, a degenerate field
V_{\langle r,s\rangle}(x)
with a vanishing descendant at level
rs
leads to a BPZ equation of order
rs
.

In the case of a three-point function (
N=3
), the dependence on
z_{i}
is already completely determined by global conformal symmetry, see Eq. (
2.2.42
).
The BPZ equation therefore leads to constraints on the conformal dimensions
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
– that is, to the fusion rules. (See Exercise
2.20
.) If
N\geq 4
, taking the global conformal symmetry into account as in Eq. (
2.2.45
), it is possible to rewrite a BPZ equation as a differential equation for a function of
N-3
variables.
In particular, a BPZ equation for a four-point function boils down to an ordinary differential equation – a differential equation for a function of a single variable.

Case of a four-point function with the field
V_{\langle 2,1\rangle}

Let us derive the ordinary differential equation for
\left\langle V_{\langle 2,1\rangle}(x)\prod_{i=1}^{3}V_{\Delta_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle
.
Inserting the identity
\oint_{\infty}dz\ \epsilon(z)T(z)=0
with
\epsilon(z)=\frac{(z-z_{1})(z-z_{2})(z-z_{3})}{z-x}
, and using Eq. (
2.3.1
), we obtain a differential equation with derivatives with respect to
x
alone,

\Bigg\{\prod_{i=1}^{3}(x-z_{i})\left(-\frac{1}{b^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{1}{x-z_{i}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial x}}\right)+(3x-z_{1}-z_{2}-z_{3})\Delta_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\\
+\frac{z_{12}z_{13}}{z_{1}-x}\Delta_{1}+\frac{z_{21}z_{23}}{z_{2}-x}\Delta_{2}+\frac{z_{31}z_{32}}{z_{3}-x}\Delta_{3}\Bigg\}\left\langle V_{\langle 2,1\rangle}(x)\prod_{i=1}^{3}V_{\Delta_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=0\ .

(2.3.25)

Setting
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=(0,\infty,1)
, this amounts to the equation

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{x(1-x)}{b^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}+(2x-1){\frac{\partial}{\partial x}}+\Delta_{\langle 2,1\rangle}+\frac{\Delta_{1}}{x}-\Delta_{2}+\frac{\Delta_{3}}{1-x}\right\}\mathcal{F}(x)=0\ ,

(2.3.26)

for

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(x)=\left\langle V_{\langle 2,1\rangle}(x)V_{\Delta_{1}}(0)V_{\Delta_{2}}(\infty)V_{\Delta_{3}}(1)\right\rangle\ .

(2.3.27)

(The
r
-th order differential equation for
\left\langle V_{\langle r,1\rangle}(x)V_{\Delta_{1}}(0)V_{\Delta_{2}}(\infty)V_{\Delta_{3}}(1)\right\rangle
can be found in
[
17
]
.)

Qualitative properties of the second-order BPZ equation

Since the equation (
2.3.26
) has coefficients that diverge at
x=0,1,\infty
, its solutions can be non-analytic at these points.
More precisely, these points are regular singular points.
A
regular singular point

x_{0}
is characterized by the existence of a basis of solutions of the type

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(x)=(x-x_{0})^{\lambda}\left(1+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}(x-x_{0})^{n}\right)\ ,

(2.3.28)

where
\lambda
is called a
characteristic exponent
.
The characteristic exponents of the equation (
2.3.26
) at the regular singular point
x=0
are obtained by inserting the ansatz
\mathcal{F}(x)=x^{\lambda}(1+O(x))
, which leads to

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}-bQ\lambda+b^{2}\Delta_{1}=0\ .

(2.3.29)

The roots of this equation have simple expressions in terms of the momentum
P_{1}
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pm}=b\left(\frac{Q}{2}\mp P_{1}\right)\ .

(2.3.30)

In conformal field theory, coincidences of fields lead to
singularities of correlation functions, with characteristic exponents that are constrained by the corresponding OPEs.
In the case of our four-point function (
2.3.27
) at
x=0
, the fusion rule (
2.3.10
) leads to an OPE of the type

\displaystyle V_{\langle 2,1\rangle}(x)V_{P}(0)=\sum_{\pm}C_{\pm}(P)x^{\Delta\left(P\pm\frac{b}{2}\right)-\Delta(P)-\Delta_{\langle 2,1\rangle}}\left(V_{P\pm\frac{b}{2}}(0)+O(x)\right)\ ,

(2.3.31)

where
C_{\pm}(P)
are some constants, and the powers of
x
are dictated by Eq. (
2.2.58
).
Computing these powers, we recover the characteristic exponents

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pm}=\Delta\left(P\pm\frac{b}{2}\right)-\Delta(P)-\Delta_{\langle 2,1\rangle}\ ,

(2.3.32)

so that each characteristic exponent corresponds to a primary field in the OPE.
(See also Exercise
2.21
.) The corresponding solutions are called
s
-channel
degenerate conformal blocks
, denoted
\mathcal{F}^{(s)}_{\pm}(x)
, and depicted as

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{(s)}_{\pm}(x)=\hbox to117.34pt{\vbox to61.36pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 40.86917pt\lower-31.09486pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(2.3.33)

where the degenerate field is represented as a dashed line.
These degenerate conformal blocks are special cases of the more general conformal blocks of Section
2.4
.

Alternative derivation from fusion rules

It turns out that the second-order BPZ equation is the only meromorphic second-order differential equation with three regular singular points at
x=0,1,\infty
, and characteristic exponents of the type (
2.3.30
). So this equation can be derived from the fusion rules, without knowing the level two null vectors. (See Exercise
2.22
.) The mathematical interpretation is that the second-order BPZ equation corresponds to a rigid Fuchsian system. Higher-order BPZ equations do not correspond to rigid Fuchsian systems: for a discussion of the theory of rigid Fuchsian systems as applied to differential equations from CFT, see
[
18
]
.

2.3.3
Hypergeometric conformal blocks

Hypergeometric equation, hypergeometric function

Let us define the
hypergeometric equation
with parameters
A,B,C
,

\displaystyle\left\{x(1-x)\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}+\left[C-(A+B+1)x\right]{\frac{\partial}{\partial x}}-AB\right\}\mathcal{G}(x)=0\ .

(2.3.34)

Using a change of parameters and unknown function,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(x)=x^{j_{1}}(x-1)^{j_{3}}\mathcal{H}(x)\ ,\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}j_{1}=-\frac{C}{2}\ ,\\
j_{2}=\frac{B-A-1}{2}\ ,\\
j_{3}=\frac{C-A-B-1}{2}\ ,\end{array}\right.\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}A=-1-j_{1}-j_{2}-j_{3}\ ,\\
B=-j_{1}+j_{2}-j_{3}\ ,\\
C=-2j_{1}\ ,\end{array}\right.

we obtain the twisted hypergeometric equation,

\displaystyle\left\{x(1-x)\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}-\frac{j_{1}(j_{1}+1)}{x}+j_{2}(j_{2}+1)+\frac{j_{3}(j_{3}+1)}{x-1}\right\}\mathcal{H}(x)=0\ .

(2.3.41)

The characteristic exponents of the hypergeometric equation at the regular singular point
x=0
are
\lambda\in\{0,1-C\}
, and the solution
\mathcal{G}(x)
that corresponds to
\lambda=0

is the
hypergeometric function

\displaystyle F(A,B,C,x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(A)_{n}(B)_{n}}{n!(C)_{n}}x^{n}\ ,

(2.3.42)

where
(A)_{n}=\frac{\Gamma(A+n)}{\Gamma(A)}
.
The solution with the characteristic exponent
\lambda=1-C
is
x^{1-C}F(A-C+1,B-C+1,2-C,x)
.
The hypergeometric function obeys the identities

\displaystyle F(A,B,C,x)=F(B,A,C,x)=(1-x)^{C-A-B}F(C-A,C-B,C,x)\ .

(2.3.43)

Solving a hypergeometric BPZ equation

We have the following equivalences of differential equations:

BPZ equation (
2.3.26
) for

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(x)

(2.3.44)

\displaystyle\iff\ \text{hypergeometric equation for\ \ }

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(x)=x^{-b(\frac{Q}{2}+P_{1})}(1-x)^{-b(\frac{Q}{2}+P_{3})}\mathcal{F}(x)

(2.3.45)

\displaystyle\iff\ \text{twisted\ hypergeometric equation for\ \ }

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(x)=(x(1-x))^{-\frac{b^{2}}{2}}\mathcal{F}(x)\ ,

(2.3.46)

provided the parameters are related as

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}A=\frac{1}{2}+b(P_{1}+P_{2}+P_{3})\ ,\\
B=\frac{1}{2}+b(P_{1}-P_{2}+P_{3})\ ,\\
C=1+2bP_{1}\ ,\end{array}\right.

or equivalently
P_{i}=-b^{-1}(j_{i}+\frac{1}{2})
.
(See Exercise
2.23
.)
Therefore, the solutions of the equation (
2.3.26
) that correspond to the characteristic exponents
\lambda_{\pm}
(
2.3.30
) at
x=0
are

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{F}^{(s)}_{-}(x)=x^{b(\frac{Q}{2}+P_{1})}(1-x)^{b(\frac{Q}{2}+P_{3})}F(A,B,C,x)\ ,\\
\mathcal{F}^{(s)}_{+}(x)=x^{b(\frac{Q}{2}-P_{1})}(1-x)^{b(\frac{Q}{2}+P_{3})}F(1-C+A,1-C+B,2-C,x)\ ,\end{array}\right.

where the superscript in
\mathcal{F}^{(s)}_{\pm}(x)
stands for the
s
-channel basis of solutions.
These formulas are consistent with the invariance of the original differential equation (
2.3.26
) under reflections of
P_{i}
: reflection of
P_{1}
exchanges the two solutions, and reflections of
P_{2}
and
P_{3}
leave each solution invariant, as follows from Eq. (
2.3.43
).
The corresponding solutions of the twisted equation (
2.3.41
) are

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{H}^{(s)}_{-}(x)=x^{-j_{1}}(1-x)^{-j_{3}}F(-j_{1}-j_{2}-j_{3}-1,-j_{1}+j_{2}-j_{3},-2j_{1},x)\ ,\\
\mathcal{H}^{(s)}_{+}(x)=x^{j_{1}+1}(1-x)^{-j_{3}}F(j_{1}-j_{2}-j_{3},j_{1}+j_{2}-j_{3}+1,2j_{1}+2,x)\ .\end{array}\right.

Fusing matrix

Let us define the
t
-channel basis of solutions, whose elements are of the type (
2.3.28
) near the regular singular point
x_{0}=1
.
Such solutions are obtained from the
s
-channel solutions by the exchanges
\left\{\begin{smallmatrix}1\leftrightarrow 3\\
x\leftrightarrow 1-x\end{smallmatrix}\right.
, and we find

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{-}(x)=x^{b(\frac{Q}{2}+P_{1})}(1-x)^{b(\frac{Q}{2}+P_{3})}F(A,B,A+B-C+1,1-x)\ ,\\
\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{+}(x)=x^{b(\frac{Q}{2}+P_{1})}(1-x)^{b(\frac{Q}{2}-P_{3})}F(C-A,C-B,C-A-B+1,1-x)\ ,\end{array}\right.

whose diagrammatic representations are

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{\pm}(x)=\hbox to94.57pt{\vbox to84.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 52.2501pt\lower-42.47578pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
