.
Deduce that the corresponding generalized minimal model is not unitary.

3.

In the minimal model case
b^{2}=-\frac{q}{p}
, show that

\displaystyle\bigcup_{r=1}^{p-1}\bigcup_{s=1}^{q-1}\left]\frac{r-1}{s-1},\frac{r+1}{s+1}\right[=\left]0,\frac{p}{p+1}\right[\ .

(3.3.21)

(Pay special attention to the case
s=r+1
.) Deduce that if
|q-p|>1
, then the corresponding minimal model is not unitary.

Exercise 3.12

(Structure constant of the
(5,2)
minimal model)

Let us compute the nontrivial three-point structure constant
\check{C}_{\phi\phi\phi}
of the
(5,2)
minimal model.

1.

Using Eq. (
3.2.35
), write

\displaystyle\check{C}_{\phi\phi\phi}=\sqrt{\frac{R_{P_{\langle 1,1\rangle}}}{R_{P_{\langle 2,1\rangle}}^{3}}}C_{\langle 2,1\rangle,\langle 2,1\rangle,\langle 3,1\rangle}\ .

(3.3.22)

2.

Notice that
C_{\langle 2,1\rangle,\langle 2,1\rangle,\langle 3,1\rangle}=C_{+}(P_{\langle 2,1\rangle})=1
due to Eqs. (
3.1.10
) and (
3.1.67
).
Choosing an appropriate value for
\mu
, compute

\displaystyle R_{P_{\langle 1,1\rangle}}=-\frac{\Gamma(-\frac{3}{5})\Gamma(\frac{3}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{5})\Gamma(-\frac{3}{2})}\ ,\quad R_{P_{\langle 2,1\rangle}}=-\frac{\Gamma(-\frac{1}{5})\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{5})\Gamma(-\frac{1}{2})}\ ,

(3.3.23)

and deduce the explicit formula (
3.2.41
) for
\check{C}_{\phi\phi\phi}
.

3.

Rederive the same result using Eq. (
3.3.19
).

Exercise 3.13

(Structure constant of the
(4,3)
minimal model)

Let us compute the nontrivial three-point structure constant
\check{C}_{\sigma\sigma\epsilon}
of the
(4,3)
minimal model.

1.

Show that

\displaystyle\check{C}_{\sigma\sigma\epsilon}=\frac{1}{R_{P_{\langle 2,1\rangle}}}\sqrt{\frac{R_{P_{\langle 1,1\rangle}}}{R_{P_{\langle 1,2\rangle}}}}C_{\langle 2,1\rangle,\langle 2,1\rangle,\langle 3,1\rangle}\ .

(3.3.24)

2.

Choosing an appropriate value for
\mu
, compute

\displaystyle R_{P_{\langle 1,1\rangle}}=-\frac{\Gamma(-\frac{1}{4})\Gamma(\frac{1}{3})}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{4})\Gamma(-\frac{1}{3})}\ ,\quad R_{P_{\langle 1,2\rangle}}=-\frac{\Gamma(-\frac{5}{4})\Gamma(\frac{5}{3})}{\Gamma(\frac{5}{4})\Gamma(-\frac{5}{3})}\ ,\quad R_{P_{\langle 2,1\rangle}}=-\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(-\frac{2}{3})}{\Gamma(-\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(\frac{2}{3})}\ ,

(3.3.25)

and deduce the explicit formula (
3.2.49
) for
\check{C}_{\sigma\sigma\epsilon}
.

3.

Rederive that structure constant using the results of Exercise
3.9
.

Chapter 4
Affine symmetry

Affine Lie algebras are infinite-dimensional Lie algebras whose universal enveloping algebras contain the Virasoro algebra.
In order to study conformal field theories based on these algebras, we will follow the same steps as in the case of the Virasoro algebra: study the algebras and their representations, deduce Ward identities and differential equations for correlation functions, and write spectrums and correlation functions for particular models.

4.1
Free bosons

4.1.1
Symmetry algebra and representations

The
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
current and the energy-momentum tensor

In Section
2.2.1
, we showed how the Virasoro symmetry algebra is encoded in the energy-momentum tensor
T(y)
.
Similarly, we now introduce the holomorphic
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
current

J(y)
, the symmetry field that encodes the symmetry algebra of free bosonic theories.
This field is characterized by the OPE

\displaystyle\boxed{J(y)J(z)=\frac{-\frac{1}{2}}{(y-z)^{2}}+O(1)}\ .

(4.1.1)

Using such a
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
current, it is possible to build a one-parameter family of Virasoro fields,

\displaystyle\boxed{T(z)=-(JJ)(z)-Q\partial J(z)}\ ,

(4.1.2)

where the parameter
Q
will shortly be found to coincide with the background charge.
Here
(JJ)(z)
is an instance of the
normal-ordered product
,

\displaystyle(AB)(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{z}\frac{dy}{y-z}A(y)B(z)\ .

(4.1.3)

Equivalently, if
\mbox{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.0pt}{1.0pt}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 3.75pt\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}\rule[-6.5pt]{3.75pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$A$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{13.0394pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$(y)$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{4.0434pt}{0.3pt}\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}}\mbox{$B$}}(z)
is the singular part of the OPE
A(y)B(z)
,
we have

\displaystyle(AB)(z)

\displaystyle=\underset{y\to z}{\lim}\left(A(y)B(z)-\mbox{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.0pt}{1.0pt}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 3.75pt\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}\rule[-6.5pt]{3.75pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$A$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{13.0394pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$(y)$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{4.0434pt}{0.3pt}\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}}\mbox{$B$}}(z)\right)\ ,

(4.1.4)

\displaystyle A(y)B(z)

\displaystyle=\mbox{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.0pt}{1.0pt}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 3.75pt\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}\rule[-6.5pt]{3.75pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$A$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{13.0394pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$(y)$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{4.0434pt}{0.3pt}\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}}\mbox{$B$}}(z)+(AB)(z)+O(y-z)\ .

(4.1.5)

In our example, this amounts to

\displaystyle(JJ)(z)=\underset{y\to z}{\lim}\left(J(y)J(z)+\frac{\frac{1}{2}}{(y-z)^{2}}\right)\ .

(4.1.6)

In general
\mbox{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.0pt}{1.0pt}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 3.75pt\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}\rule[-6.5pt]{3.75pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$A$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{13.0394pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$(y)$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{4.0434pt}{0.3pt}\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}}\mbox{$B$}}(z)\neq\mbox{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.0pt}{1.0pt}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 4.0434pt\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}\rule[-6.5pt]{4.0434pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$B$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{12.8681pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$(z)$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{3.75pt}{0.3pt}\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}}\mbox{$A$}}(y)
and
(AB)\neq(BA)
although
A(y)B(z)=B(z)A(y)
.
(See Exercise
4.1
for the computation of
(AB)-(BA)
.)
OPEs of normal-ordered products can be computed using
Wick’s theorem
,

\displaystyle\mbox{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.0pt}{1.0pt}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 3.75pt\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}\rule[-6.5pt]{3.75pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$A$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{12.8681pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$(z)$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{11.86354pt}{0.3pt}\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}}\mbox{$(BC)$}}(y)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{y}\frac{dx}{x-y}\left(\mbox{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.0pt}{1.0pt}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 3.75pt\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}\rule[-6.5pt]{3.75pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$A$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{12.8681pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$(z)$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{4.0434pt}{0.3pt}\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}}\mbox{$B$}}(x)C(y)+B(x)\mbox{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.0pt}{1.0pt}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 3.75pt\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}\rule[-6.5pt]{3.75pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$A$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{12.8681pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$(z)$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{3.93124pt}{0.3pt}\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}}\mbox{$C$}}(y)\right)\ .

(4.1.7)

For example, we can compute

\displaystyle(JJ)(y)J(z)=-\frac{J(y)}{(y-z)^{2}}+O(1)=-{\frac{\partial}{\partial z}}\frac{J(z)}{y-z}+O(1)\ ,

(4.1.8)

which leads to

\displaystyle\boxed{T(y)J(z)=\frac{-Q}{(y-z)^{3}}+{\frac{\partial}{\partial z}}\frac{1}{y-z}J(z)+O(1)}\ .

(4.1.9)

Then we can compute the OPE
T(y)T(z)
, and we find nothing but the Virasoro field OPE (
2.2.11
), where the central charge is given in terms of the background charge
Q
by
c=1+6Q^{2}
(repeating Eq. (
2.1.22
)).
So
T(z)
is a Virasoro field as announced.
In order to build conformal field theories from the current
J(z)
, we choose a value of
Q
and identify the corresponding Virasoro field with the energy-momentum tensor – the Virasoro fields with
Q^{\prime}\neq Q
will henceforth play no role.

The affine Lie algebra
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}

Let us introduce the modes
J_{n}^{(z_{0})}
of the current
J(y)
,

\displaystyle J_{n}^{(z_{0})}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{z_{0}}dy\ (y-z_{0})^{n}J(y)\ .

(4.1.10)

Equivalently, the OPE of
J(y)
with a generic field
V_{\sigma}(z_{0})
is

\displaystyle\boxed{J(y)V_{\sigma}(z_{0})=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\frac{J_{n}V_{\sigma}(z_{0})}{(y-z_{0})^{n+1}}}\ ,

(4.1.11)

where we use the simplified notation
J_{n}=J_{n}^{(z_{0})}
when working at fixed
z_{0}
.
This OPE is consistent with dimensional analysis, if in addition to the known dimensions (
2.2.13
) we assume
[J]=1
and
[J_{n}]=-n
.
The
JJ
OPE (
4.1.1
) is equivalent to the commutation relations

\displaystyle\boxed{[J_{m},J_{n}]=\frac{1}{2}n\delta_{m+n,0}}\ ,

(4.1.12)

which define the affine Lie algebra
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
.
This algebra can be written as a direct sum of commuting finite-dimensional subalgebras as

\displaystyle\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}=\text{Span}(J_{0})\oplus\bigoplus_{n=1}^{\infty}\text{Span}(J_{n},J_{-n})\ .

(4.1.13)

The construction (
4.1.2
) of
T(z)
from the
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
current
J(z)
is equivalent to the following formula for the modes
L_{n}
(
2.2.7
) of
T(z)
:

\displaystyle L_{n}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}}J_{n-m}J_{m}+Q(n+1)J_{n}\ ,\qquad(n\neq 0)\ ,

(4.1.14)

\displaystyle L_{0}

\displaystyle=-2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}J_{-m}J_{m}-J_{0}^{2}+QJ_{0}\ ,

(4.1.15)

and the
TJ
OPE (
4.1.9
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle[L_{m},J_{n}]=-nJ_{m+n}-\frac{Q}{2}m(m+1)\delta_{m+n,0}\ .

(4.1.16)

Let us briefly discuss how
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
generators can behave under Hermitian conjugation.
It turns out that a conjugation of
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
that is compatible with the conjugation (
1.4.5
) of the Virasoro algebra can exist only if
Q^{2}\in\mathbb{R}
(see Exercise
4.2
).
The conjugation is then

\displaystyle J_{n}^{\dagger}=\operatorname{sign}(Q^{2})\big(Q\delta_{n,0}-J_{-n}\big)\ ,\qquad(Q^{2}\in\mathbb{R})\ .

(4.1.17)

Primary fields and highest-weight representations

We define a
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
-primary field

V_{\alpha}(z_{0})
with the
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
momentum

\alpha
by its OPE with
J(y)
,

\displaystyle\boxed{J(y)V_{\alpha}(z_{0})=\frac{\alpha}{y-z_{0}}V_{\alpha}(z_{0})+O(1)}\ .

(4.1.18)

To a
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
-primary field
V_{\alpha}(z_{0})
we associate a highest-weight representation
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
of the affine Lie algebra
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
.
The states in
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
correspond to
V_{\alpha}(z_{0})
and its
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
-descendant fields
V_{\sigma}(z_{0})=\left(\prod_{i}J_{-n_{i}}\right)V_{\alpha}(z_{0})
with
n_{i}\geq 1
, which all obey

\displaystyle\left(J_{0}-\alpha\right)V_{\sigma}(z_{0})=0

(4.1.19)

For
Q^{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,
a natural scalar product can be defined in
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
using Eq. (
4.1.17
).
Then, if
|\alpha\rangle
is the
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
-primary state of
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
, we have
\left<J_{-1}\alpha\middle|J_{-1}\alpha\right>=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{sign}(Q^{2})\langle\alpha|\alpha\rangle
.
So, for the scalar product to be positive definite, we need
Q\in\mathbb{R}
.
Moreover, since
J_{0}
obeys
J_{0}^{\dagger}=Q-J_{0}
and has the eigenvalue
\alpha
, we must have
\alpha\in\frac{Q}{2}+i{\mathbb{R}}
.

Any
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
-primary field is also a primary field, whose OPE
T(y)V_{\alpha}(z_{0})
is given by Eq. (
2.2.9
), with the conformal dimension

\displaystyle\Delta(\alpha)=\alpha(Q-\alpha)\ .

(4.1.20)

Comparing with
Eq. (
2.1.27
), we obtain the relation
\alpha=\frac{Q}{2}+P
(up to reflection) between
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
and Virasoro momentums.
Under the action of the Virasoro algebra Eqs. (
4.1.14
)-(
4.1.15
), the
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
highest-weight representation
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
coincides with a Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{P}
of momentum
P=\pm(\alpha-\frac{Q}{2})
, provided this Verma module is irreducible. (For the case of reducible Verma modules, see Exercise
4.3
.)
To summarize,

Symmetry algebra

\mathfrak{V}

\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}

Existence of scalar product

Q\in{\mathbb{R}}\cup i{\mathbb{R}}

Q\in{\mathbb{R}}\cup i{\mathbb{R}}

Unitarity

Q\in\mathbb{R}
and
P\in i{\mathbb{R}}\cup]-\frac{Q}{2},\frac{Q}{2}[

Q\in\mathbb{R}
and
\alpha\in\frac{Q}{2}+i{\mathbb{R}}

Reflection relation

\mathcal{V}_{P}=\mathcal{V}_{-P}

\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\neq\mathcal{U}_{Q-\alpha}

Even in theories with no
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
symmetry (and therefore no
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
Ward identities),
it can be useful to think of Virasoro representations as
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
representations.
For example,
whenever Liouville theory is unitary i.e.
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
, its spectrum (
3.1.2
) contains only representations that are unitary as
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
representations, leaving out the representations with
P\in]-\frac{Q}{2},\frac{Q}{2}[
although they are unitary as Virasoro representations.
For example, the AGT relation leads to an expression for Virasoro conformal blocks as sums over particular descendant states, and these states simplify when written in terms of
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
creation modes
[
23
]
.

4.1.2
Ward identities and Seiberg–Witten equations

Ward identities

In order to derive
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
Ward identities, we need to know how the current
J(y)
behaves as
y\to\infty
.
The relation (
4.1.2
) with the energy-momentum tensor
T(y)
, and the behaviour (
2.2.10
) of
T(y)
, suggest

\displaystyle\boxed{J(y)\underset{y\to\infty}{=}\frac{Q}{y}+O\left(\frac{1}{y^{2}}\right)}\ .

(4.1.25)

For any meromorphic function
\epsilon(y)
, with no poles outside
\{z_{1},\cdots z_{N}\}
, we have

\displaystyle\oint_{\infty}dy\ \epsilon(y)\left\langle\left(J(y)-\frac{Q}{y}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{N}V_{\sigma_{j}}(z_{j})\right\rangle=0\quad\text{provided}\quad\epsilon(y)\underset{y\to\infty}{=}O(1)\ .

(4.1.26)

In the case
\epsilon(y)=1
, we obtain the
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
global Ward identity.
Assuming all fields are primary or descendant fields, and therefore obey Eq. (
4.1.19
), the global Ward identity reads

\displaystyle\boxed{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}=Q}\ ,

(4.1.27)

In the case
\epsilon(z)=\frac{1}{(z-z_{i})^{n}}
with
n\geq 1
, we obtain the
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
local Ward identity

\displaystyle\left\langle\left(J_{-n}^{(z_{i})}+(-1)^{n}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{\binom{p+n-1}{p}}{z_{ij}^{n+p}}J_{p}^{(z_{j})}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{N}V_{\sigma_{j}}(z_{j})\right\rangle=0\ .

(4.1.28)

This simplifies when the fields with indices
j\neq i
are primary,

\displaystyle\left\langle\left(J_{-n}^{(z_{i})}+\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{\alpha_{j}}{z_{ji}^{n}}\right)V_{\sigma_{i}}(z_{i})\prod_{j\neq i}V_{\alpha_{j}}(z_{j})\right\rangle=0\ .

(4.1.29)

If all fields are primary, we actually have

\displaystyle\boxed{\left\langle J(y)\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\alpha_{i}}{y-z_{i}}\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle}\ .

(4.1.30)

OPEs and fusion rule

Since
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
-primary fields are also Virasoro-primary, the OPE of two left and right

\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
-primary fields must be of the type

\displaystyle V_{\alpha_{1},\bar{\alpha}_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\alpha_{2},\bar{\alpha}_{2}}(z_{2})=\sum_{\alpha_{3},\bar{\alpha}_{3}}C_{12}^{3}\left|z_{12}^{\Delta(\alpha_{3})-\Delta(\alpha_{1})-\Delta(\alpha_{2})}\right|^{2}\Big(V_{\alpha_{3},\bar{\alpha}_{3}}(z_{2})+O(z_{12})\Big)\ .

(4.1.31)

Inserting
\oint_{\infty}J(z)dz
on both sides of this OPE, we obtain a factor
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
on the left, and a factor
\alpha_{3}
on the right.
This leads to
(\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})C_{12}^{3}=0
, so that
V_{\alpha_{3},\bar{\alpha}_{3}}
can appear only if
\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
.
In other words, we have the
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
fusion rule

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{1}}\times\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{2}}=\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}}\ ,

(4.1.32)

i.e. the
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
momentum
\alpha
is conserved.
The OPE then reads

\displaystyle\boxed{V_{\alpha_{1},\bar{\alpha}_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\alpha_{2},\bar{\alpha}_{2}}(z_{2})=\left|z_{12}^{-2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\right|^{2}\big(V_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\bar{\alpha}_{2}}(z_{2})+O(z_{12})\big)}\ ,

(4.1.33)

where we have normalized the fields so that the OPE coefficient is one. (See Exercise
4.4
.)
It is actually possible to write all the descendants explicitly,

\displaystyle V_{\alpha_{1},\bar{\alpha}_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\alpha_{2},\bar{\alpha}_{2}}(z_{2})=\left|z_{12}^{-2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\exp\left(-\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}\frac{2}{p}\alpha_{1}z_{12}^{p}J_{-p}\right)\right|^{2}V_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},\bar{\alpha}_{1}+\bar{\alpha}_{2}}(z_{2})\ .

(4.1.34)

Differential equations

The conformal symmetry equations (
2.2.23
) of course still hold in theories with the
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
symmetry algebra.
But the construction (
4.1.2
) of
T(z)
from the
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
current
J(z)
leads to additional differential equations.
In order to derive them, let us consider

\displaystyle{\frac{\partial}{\partial z}}V_{\alpha}(z)=L_{-1}V_{\alpha}(z)=-2J_{-1}J_{0}V_{\alpha}(z)\ ,

(4.1.35)

where we used Eq. (
4.1.14
) for expressing
L_{-1}
in terms of
J_{-1}
and
J_{0}
.
We therefore obtain

\displaystyle{\frac{\partial}{\partial z}}V_{\alpha}(z)

\displaystyle=-2\alpha J_{-1}V_{\alpha}(z)=-2\alpha(JV_{\alpha})(z)\ .

(4.1.36)

Applying this to a field
V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})
in an

N
-point function of
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
-primary fields, and using
the local Ward identity (
4.1.29
) for
J_{-1}^{(z_{i})}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left({\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}}+\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{2\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}}{z_{i}-z_{j}}\right)\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=0\ .

(4.1.37)

This implies

\displaystyle\boxed{\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle\propto\prod_{i<j}(z_{i}-z_{j})^{-2\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}}}\ ,

(4.1.38)

where the proportionality factor is an arbitrary antiholomorphic function of the positions.
In particular, the left and right
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
-primary field
V_{0,0}(z)
must be
z
-independent.
In the normalization of Eq. (
4.1.33
),
V_{0,0}(z)
is actually the identity field.

Seiberg–Witten equations

Let us use Eq. (
4.1.36
) to find out how primary fields depend on
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
momentums.
Since conformal spins take integer values, we cannot vary left and right momentums independently, and we will consider a diagonal primary field
V_{\alpha,\alpha}(z)
.
If
\nabla_{z}={\frac{\partial}{\partial z}}+2\alpha J(z)
and
\overline{\nabla}_{z}={\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}}}+2\alpha\bar{J}(z)
, then
\nabla_{z}V_{\alpha,\alpha}(z)=\overline{\nabla}_{z}V_{\alpha,\alpha}(z)=0
, neglecting the issue of regularizing operator products.
If we then define
\nabla_{\alpha}={\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}}+2\int^{z}(J(y)dy+\bar{J}(y)d\bar{y})
so that
[\nabla_{z},\nabla_{\alpha}]=[\overline{\nabla}_{z},\nabla_{\alpha}]=0
, then
\nabla_{\alpha}V_{\alpha,\alpha}(z)
is annihilated by
\nabla_{z},\overline{\nabla}_{z}
, and must be proportional to
V_{\alpha,\alpha}(z)
.
The proportionality constant can be set to zero by renormalizing the field
V_{\alpha,\alpha}(z)
, and this normalization coincides with the normalization that we adopted in Eq. (
4.1.33
).
We thus obtain

\displaystyle{\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}}V_{\alpha,\alpha}(z)=-2\int^{z}V_{\alpha,\alpha}(z)\Big(J(y)dy+\bar{J}(y)d\bar{y}\Big)\ ,

(4.1.39)

where the integral denotes a regularized value of a primitive of the integrand. (The integrand is a closed one-form because
\bar{\partial}J=\partial\bar{J}=0
.)
It can be checked that this equation holds when inserted into correlation functions of primary fields, using Eqs. (
4.1.38
) and (
4.1.30
), provided we use the primitive
\int^{z}\frac{dy}{y-z_{i}}=\log(z-z_{i})
.
From the OPE (
4.1.18
), we also have the equation

\displaystyle\alpha V_{\alpha,\alpha}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{z}V_{\alpha,\alpha}(z)J(y)dy=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{z}V_{\alpha,\alpha}(z)\bar{J}(y)d\bar{y}\ .

(4.1.40)

Equations (
4.1.39
) and (
4.1.40
) show that the actions of
{\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}}
and
\alpha
on
V_{\alpha,\alpha}(z)
can be obtained by integrating field-valued one-forms along particular contours.
By analogy with supersymmetric gauge theory, these equations may be called
Seiberg--Witten equations
.

4.1.3
Spectrum and correlation functions

We define a
free bosonic theory
as a conformal field theory with
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
symmetry, such that each representation of the
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}\times\bar{\hat{\mathfrak{u}}}_{1}
symmetry algebra appears at most once in the spectrum. Let us parametrize highest-weight representations of
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}\times\bar{\hat{\mathfrak{u}}}_{1}
by their momentum vectors
\vec{\alpha}=(\alpha,\bar{\alpha})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
: by closure under fusion (
4.1.32
), the set of the momentum vectors must close under addition. Moreover, we assume that the corresponding spins are integer,

\displaystyle S(\vec{\alpha})=(\alpha-\bar{\alpha})(\alpha+\bar{\alpha}-Q)\in\mathbb{Z}\ .

(4.1.41)

Let us analyze the interplay between this integer spin condition and closure under fusion, depending on whether the background charge vanishes or not.

Discrete spectrums with
Q=0

For
Q=0
, arbitrary integer linear combinations of two momentum vectors have integer spins if and only if the vectors and their sums have integer spins,

\displaystyle S\left(\mathbb{Z}\vec{\alpha}_{1}+\mathbb{Z}\vec{\alpha}_{2}\right)\subset\mathbb{Z}\quad\iff\quad S\left(\left\{\vec{\alpha}_{1},\vec{\alpha}_{2},\vec{\alpha}_{1}+\vec{\alpha}_{2}\right\}\right)\subset\mathbb{Z}\ .

(4.1.42)

More generally, the elements of the spectrum
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{Z}\vec{\alpha}_{i}
have integer spins if and only if the vectors
\vec{\alpha}_{i}
and
\vec{\alpha}_{i}+\vec{\alpha}_{j}
(with
i\neq j
) have integer spins. This gives us
\frac{1}{2}N(N+1)
constraints for the
2N
complex components of the vectors
\vec{\alpha}_{i}
. We focus on spectrums where these constraints are underdetermined and leave us with at least one continuous parameter, i.e.
N=1,2
: we call these spectrums generic. We further focus on spectrums that cannot be extended by adding an extra vector, i.e.
N=2
: we call these spectrums generic and maximal.

A generic maximal spectrum may or may not contain diagonal states, see Exercise
4.5
. We focus on generic maximal spectrums that do, and we parametrize a diagonal generator as
\vec{\alpha}_{1}=(\frac{i}{2R},\frac{i}{2R})
. For
\vec{\alpha}_{2}
another generator, we have

\displaystyle S(\vec{\alpha}_{1}+\vec{\alpha}_{2})-S(\vec{\alpha}_{1})-S(\vec{\alpha}_{2})=\frac{i}{R}(\alpha_{2}-\bar{\alpha}_{2})\ .

(4.1.43)

This combination of spins must be integer, and since the spectrum is maximal we have
\alpha_{2}-\bar{\alpha}_{2}=iR
(or equivalently
\alpha_{2}-\bar{\alpha}_{2}=-iR
). This implies
\alpha_{2}+\bar{\alpha}_{2}\in\frac{i}{R}\mathbb{Z}
,
and by doing a redefinition of the type
\vec{\alpha}_{2}\to\vec{\alpha}_{2}+k\vec{\alpha}_{1}
with
k\in\mathbb{Z}
we set
\alpha_{2}+\bar{\alpha}_{2}=0
and therefore
\vec{\alpha}_{2}=(\frac{iR}{2},-\frac{iR}{2})
. This leads to the spectrum

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{S}_{R}=\bigoplus_{(n,w)\in{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}}\mathcal{U}_{\frac{i}{2}\left(\frac{n}{R}+Rw\right)}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{U}}_{\frac{i}{2}\left(\frac{n}{R}-Rw\right)}}\ .

(4.1.44)

The corresponding theory is called the
compactified free boson
theory, whose parameter
R
is the compactification radius.
The integers
n
and
w
are respectively called the momentum and winding number.
Free bosons are essential building blocks of string theory, which is at the origin of the names for
R
,
n
and
w
.

The algebra
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
has the automorphism

\displaystyle\omega(J_{n})=-J_{n}\ ,

(4.1.45)

which preserves the Virasoro generators (
4.1.14
)-(
4.1.15
). (For
Q\neq 0
we would have to assume
\omega(Q)=-Q
.)
This automorphism acts on representations of
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}\times\bar{\hat{\mathfrak{u}}}_{1}
as
\omega(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{U}}_{\bar{\alpha}})=\mathcal{U}_{-\alpha}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{U}}_{\bar{\alpha}}
, and on the spectrum of a compactified free boson as

\displaystyle\omega(\mathcal{S}_{R})=\mathcal{S}_{\frac{1}{R}}\ .

(4.1.46)

This shows that two compactified free bosons are equivalent if their radiuses are inverses of one another. In string theory, this is called T-duality.

Discrete spectrums with
Q\neq 0

For
Q\neq 0
,
S(\vec{\alpha})\in\mathbb{Z}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}S(2\vec{\alpha})\in\mathbb{Z}
, due to the identity

\displaystyle S(2\vec{\alpha})-4S(\vec{\alpha})=2Q(\alpha-\bar{\alpha})\ .

(4.1.47)

Looking again for a generic maximal spectrum of the type
\mathbb{Z}\vec{\alpha}_{1}+\mathbb{Z}\vec{\alpha}_{2}
, we must have

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}-\bar{\alpha}_{i}\in\frac{1}{2Q}\mathbb{Z}\ .

(4.1.48)

This implies that the spectrum contains diagonal states. And actually, the compactified free boson spectrum
\mathcal{S}_{R}
(
4.1.44
) is made of states with integer spins provided

\displaystyle R\in\frac{1}{iQ}\mathbb{Z}\ .

(4.1.49)

In this spectrum however, we have
\alpha-\bar{\alpha}\in\frac{1}{Q}\mathbb{Z}
.
If we insisted that the constraint (
4.1.48
) be saturated, then we would find the alternative spectrum

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{R}=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigoplus_{w\in\frac{n}{2}+\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{U}_{\frac{i}{2}\left(\frac{n}{R}+Rw\right)}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{U}}_{\frac{i}{2}\left(\frac{n}{R}-Rw\right)}\qquad\text{with}\qquad R\in\frac{1}{iQ}(2\mathbb{Z}+1)\ .

(4.1.50)

Continuous spectrums

The large
R
limit of the compactified free boson spectrum (
4.1.44
) is a continuous, diagonal spectrum.
If
Q\neq 0
, the compactification radius
R
must obey the condition (
4.1.49
), and we can send it to infinity in a particular,
Q
-dependent direction. We find the spectrum

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{S}=\int_{Q\mathbb{R}}d\alpha\ \mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}}\ .

(4.1.51)

For
Q=0
we can take
Q\mathbb{R}
to be a straight line in an arbitrary direction, although the direction
\alpha\in i\mathbb{R}
is singled out by unitarity.
The resulting theory is called the (uncompactified)
free boson
theory.
In the context of string theory, this theory is also called the linear dilaton theory if
Q\neq 0
.

Unitarity

The only unitary free bosonic theories are the compactified free boson at
Q=0
with a real compactification radius, and the uncompactified free boson that is obtained as its infinite radius limit. For
Q\neq 0
, free bosonic theories cannot be unitary, because the set
U=\frac{Q}{2}+i\mathbb{R}
of unitary
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
momentums is not closed under fusion, i.e.
(U+U)\cap U=\emptyset
if
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
.

Correlation functions

The correlation functions of our free bosonic theories with discrete spectrums have the form

\displaystyle\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i},\bar{\alpha}_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=\delta_{\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}-Q}\delta_{\sum_{i}\bar{\alpha}_{i}-Q}\prod_{i<j}(z_{i}-z_{j})^{-2\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}}(\bar{z}_{i}-\bar{z}_{j})^{-2\bar{\alpha}_{i}\bar{\alpha}_{j}}\ ,

(4.1.52)

where the two Kronecker deltas constrain the two discrete coordinates of
(\sum_{i}\alpha_{i},\sum_{i}\bar{\alpha}_{i})
.
In theories with continuous, diagonal spectrums,
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
momentum conservation is enforced by a Dirac delta function,

\displaystyle\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=\delta\left({\textstyle\sum}_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}-Q\right)\ \prod_{i<j}|z_{i}-z_{j}|^{-4\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}}\ .

(4.1.53)

In the application to Liouville theory (Section
4.1.4
), we will actually use these correlation functions for
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
momentums that do not necessarily belong to the spectrum (
4.1.51
).

While the structure constants are trivial if the spectrum only involves even spins, they are nontrivial functions with values in
\{-1,1\}
in the presence of odd spins, due to the permutation rule (
2.2.54
). In the case of a compactified free boson, the two- and three-point structure constants are

\displaystyle B_{(n_{1},w_{1})}=(-1)^{n_{1}w_{1}}\quad,\quad C_{(n_{1},w_{1}),(n_{2},w_{2}),(n_{3},w_{3})}=(-1)^{n_{1}w_{2}+n_{2}w_{3}+n_{3}w_{1}}\ .

(4.1.54)

(The Kronecker delta functions that appear in the two- and three-point functions are universal quantities, so we do not include them in the structure constants.)

4.1.4
Free bosons and Liouville theory

Let us invert the
construction (
4.1.2
) of a Virasoro field from a
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
current, and construct fields
J,\bar{J}
from the energy-momentum tensors
T,\bar{T}
of an arbitrary conformal field theory.
These fields are in general not meromorphic, so they are not symmetry fields.
Nevertheless, we will use them for computing limits and particular values of correlation functions in Liouville theory.

The Liouville equation

Equation (
4.1.2
) is not enough for determining
J
from
T
. We could add the condition that
J
be holomorphic, but we will add the milder condition
\partial\bar{J}=\bar{\partial}J
,
which is sufficient for the Seiberg–Witten equation (
4.1.39
) to make sense. This implies that locally there is a field
\phi
such that

\displaystyle J=-\partial\phi\qquad,\qquad\bar{J}=-\bar{\partial}\phi\ .

(4.1.55)

This field is called the
Liouville field
. By formally solving the Seiberg–Witten equation, we can express primary fields in terms of the Liouville field as

\displaystyle V_{\alpha}=e^{2\alpha\phi}\ .

(4.1.56)

We are now labelling primary fields using the
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
momentum
\alpha
, which is related to the conformal dimension by Eq. (
4.1.20
), and to the momentum
P
by
\alpha=\frac{Q}{2}-P
.

Given an
N
-point function
\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle
in Liouville theory, we define

\displaystyle\boxed{F=-\log\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle}\quad,\quad\boxed{W(y)=\frac{\left\langle J(y)\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle}{\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle}}\ .

(4.1.57)

In terms of these objects, the Seiberg–Witten equation (
4.1.39
) becomes

\displaystyle{\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha_{i}}}F=2\int^{z_{i}}\Big(W(y)dy+\bar{W}(y)d\bar{y}\Big)\ .

(4.1.58)

We will use these objects for studying Liouville theory in the limit
c\to\infty
– equivalently,
Q\to\infty
.
Equations such as (
4.1.25
) suggest
J=O(Q)
which leads to
W=O(Q)
.
Using Eq. (
4.1.58
), we deduce
F=O(Q^{2})
.
The natural expansion parameter is
Q^{2}
(as in
c=1+6Q^{2}
) and we write the expansions

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=Q^{2}F^{(0)}+F^{(1)}+O(Q^{-2})\ ,

(4.1.59)

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=QW^{(0)}+O(Q^{-1})\ .

(4.1.60)

Now, in the
Q\to\infty
limit of the
JJ
OPE (
4.1.1
) (at fixed positions), the singular term becomes negligible. So, in this limit, the correlation function
\left\langle J(y_{1})J(y_{2})\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle
has no singularity at
y_{1}=y_{2}
. If this correlation function was determined by the behaviour of
J
near its singularities, it would have to factorize,

\displaystyle\frac{\left\langle J(y_{1})J(y_{2})\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle}{\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle}\underset{Q\to\infty}{=}Q^{2}W^{(0)}(y_{1})W^{(0)}(y_{2})+O(1)\ .

(4.1.61)

However, since
J(y)
is not a symmetry field, it is not necessarily determined by its singular behaviour. For the moment, we assume that the factorization holds.
Inserting the definition (
4.1.2
) of
J(z)
in
\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle
then yields a nonlinear differential equation for
W^{(0)}
,

\displaystyle\boxed{\left(W^{(0)}\right)^{2}+\partial W^{(0)}=-t}\ ,

(4.1.62)

where we introduced

\displaystyle t(z)=\underset{Q\to\infty}{\lim}Q^{-2}\frac{\left\langle T(z)\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle}{\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle}\ .

(4.1.63)

This can be computed using Eq. (
2.2.17
),

\displaystyle t(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{\underset{Q\to\infty}{\lim}Q^{-2}\Delta(\alpha_{i})}{(z-z_{i})^{2}}+\frac{\beta_{i}}{z-z_{i}}\right)\ ,

(4.1.64)

where we defined the accessory parameters

\displaystyle\beta_{i}=-{\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}}F^{(0)}\ .

(4.1.65)

The condition
t(z)\underset{z\to\infty}{=}O(\frac{1}{z^{4}})
, which follows from Eq. (
2.2.10
), amounts to three constraints on the accessory parameters, which are thereby uniquely determined in the case
N=3
. Studying the behaviour of
t(z)\prod_{i=1}^{3}(z-z_{i})
near
z=z_{i}
and
z=\infty
, we find in this case

\displaystyle t(z)\underset{N=3}{=}\frac{1}{\prod_{i=1}^{3}(z-z_{i})}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{\prod_{j\neq i}z_{ij}}{z-z_{i}}\underset{Q\to\infty}{\lim}Q^{-2}\Delta(\alpha_{i})\ .

(4.1.66)

Let us reformulate our nonlinear equation for
W^{(0)}
in terms of the functions
\psi
and
\phi^{\text{cl}}
such that

\displaystyle W^{(0)}=\frac{\partial\psi}{\psi}=-\partial\phi^{\text{cl}}\qquad,\qquad\bar{W}^{(0)}=\frac{\bar{\partial}\psi}{\psi}=-\bar{\partial}\phi^{\text{cl}}\ .

(4.1.67)

In terms of
\psi
, we obtain the linear differential equation

\displaystyle\boxed{(\partial^{2}+t)\psi=0}\ .

(4.1.68)

In terms of
\phi^{\text{cl}}
, the
\bar{z}
-derivative of Eq. (
4.1.62
) reads
\partial\left(\log\partial\bar{\partial}\phi^{\text{cl}}-2\phi^{\text{cl}}\right)=0
. Together with
\bar{\partial}\left(\log\partial\bar{\partial}\phi^{\text{cl}}-2\phi^{\text{cl}}\right)=0
, this leads to

\displaystyle\partial\bar{\partial}\phi^{\text{cl}}=\mu e^{2\phi^{\text{cl}}}\ ,

(4.1.69)

where the integration constant
\mu
coincides with the cosmological constant up to a normalization factor.
This equation for
\phi^{\text{cl}}
is the
Liouville equation
, which gives its name to Liouville theory.
This equation is equivalent to the condition that the two-dimensional metric

\displaystyle ds^{2}=e^{2\phi^{\text{cl}}}dzd\bar{z}\ ,

(4.1.70)

has a constant scalar curvature.
Since all two-dimensional metrics can be brought to this form by changes of coordinates, Liouville theory can be interpreted as a quantum theory of two-dimensional gravity, whose classical equation of motion is the Liouville equation.

Heavy asymptotic limit

In order to define and compute large
c
limits of correlation functions, we should specify how the momentums behave.
Since
J=O(Q)
, it is natural to assume
\alpha_{i}=O(Q)
, i.e. to consider the

\displaystyle\textbf{\boldmath heavy\ asymptotic\ limit}{\@index{\@indexphrase{heavy\ asymptotic\ limit}}}:\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}Q\to\infty\ ,\\
\eta_{i}=\frac{\alpha_{i}}{Q}\ \text{fixed}\ .\end{array}\right.

We then have
\underset{Q\to\infty}{\lim}Q^{-2}\Delta(\alpha_{i})=\eta_{i}(1-\eta_{i})
, and Eq. (
4.1.62
) allows two possible asymptotic behaviours
W^{(0)}(y)\underset{y\to z_{1}}{=}\frac{\eta_{1}}{y-z_{1}}+O(1)
and

W^{(0)}(y)\underset{y\to z_{1}}{=}\frac{1-\eta_{1}}{y-z_{1}}+O(1)
.
In the case
N=3
, the linear differential equation (
4.1.68
) for
\psi
is equivalent to the hypergeometric equation, which has a unique single-valued solution (up to an overall constant factor).
The uniqueness of the solution provides an a posteriori justification for our factorization hypothesis (
4.1.61
).
We can then deduce the leading term
F^{(0)}
of
F
, with the help of the heavy asymptotic limit of the Seiberg–Witten equation (
4.1.58
),

\displaystyle{\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta_{i}}}F^{(0)}=2\int^{z_{i}}\Big(W^{(0)}(y)dy+\bar{W}^{(0)}(y)d\bar{y}\Big)=-2\phi^{\text{cl}}(z_{i})\ .

(4.1.73)

We do not carry out these calculations here, and instead refer the reader to
[
30
]
, where the result for
F^{(0)}
is shown to agree with the leading behaviour of the three-point function (
3.1.41
) in the heavy asymptotic limit.
Actually, the agreement does not stop at the leading order: similar techniques can be used for computing
F
order by order in
Q^{2}

[
40
]
.

Light asymptotic limit

Let us now assume that conformal dimensions are fixed as the central charge goes to infinity.
This defines the

\displaystyle\textbf{\boldmath light\ asymptotic\ limit}{\@index{\@indexphrase{light\ asymptotic\ limit}}}:\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}Q\to\infty\ ,\\
\eta_{i}=Q\alpha_{i}\ \text{fixed}\ .\end{array}\right.

The Seiberg–Witten equation (
4.1.58
) now implies

\displaystyle{\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta_{i}}}F^{(0)}=0\qquad,\qquad{\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta_{i}}}F^{(1)}

\displaystyle=-2\phi^{\text{cl}}(z_{i})\ .

(4.1.76)

So
F^{(0)}
is
\eta_{i}
-independent.
It is actually also
z_{i}
-independent, according to the following argument: since
\Delta(\alpha_{i})=\eta_{i}+O(Q^{-2})
, its is natural to assume
t(z)=0
where
t(z)
is given by Eq. (
4.1.64
), and this implies
\beta_{i}=-{\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}}F^{(0)}=0
.
So
F^{(0)}
is not affected by the presence of the fields
V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})
, which are then called light fields.
The first interesting term of
F
is thus
F^{(1)}
.
To compute
F^{(1)}
, let us solve the linear differential equation (
4.1.68
), which is now simply
\partial^{2}\psi=0
.
Together with
\bar{\partial}^{2}\psi=0
, this leads to the solutions

\psi_{h}(z)=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}z\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right]^{\dagger}h\left[\begin{smallmatrix}z\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right]
, for
h
a constant matrix such that
h=h^{\dagger}
and
\det h=-\mu
, where
\mu
is the parameter of the Liouville equation.

So the solution for
\psi
is not unique. And individual solutions are not covariant under global conformal transformations, although of course the space of solutions is invariant.
Different solutions
\psi_{h}
correspond to different linear forms on tuples of fields
(V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i}))\mapsto e^{-F_{h}}=\langle\prod_{i}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\rangle_{h}
.
Liouville correlation functions correspond to a linear form that is covariant under global conformal transformations, and does not obey the factorization assumption (
4.1.61
).
This form can be obtained as a linear combination of the linear forms
\langle\rangle_{h}
, so that
e^{-F^{(1)}}=\int dh\,e^{-F^{(1)}_{h}}=\int dh\prod_{i=1}^{N}\psi_{h}(z_{i})^{-2\eta_{i}}
.
More explicitly, in the case where the integration constant in Eq. (
4.1.69
) is
\mu=-1
, we have

\displaystyle\underset{\text{light asymptotic}}{\lim}\ \left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=\int_{H^{+}_{3}}dh\ \prod_{i=1}^{N}\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}z_{i}\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right]^{\dagger}h\left[\begin{smallmatrix}z_{i}\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right)^{-2\eta_{i}}\ ,

(4.1.77)

where the integral is over the space
H_{3}^{+}
of Hermitian matrices of size two and determinant one, with a measure
dh
that we assume to be invariant under
h\mapsto g^{\dagger}hg
for
g\in SL_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
.
In the case
N=3
, this agrees with the DOZZ formula for the three-point function (
3.1.41
). (See
[
30
]
.) In the light asymptotic limit, the spectrum of Liouville theory reduces to the space of functions on
H_{3}^{+}
, and the symmetry algebra reduces to the algebra of the global conformal transformations.
So the light asymptotic limit of Liouville theory is a two-dimensional global conformal field theory, which also describes harmonic analysis on
H_{3}^{+}
. (See Exercise
4.6
for more details.)

This relation between harmonic analysis and conformal field theory can be generalized to higher-dimensional CFT
[
54
]
and to the light asymptotic limit of 2d CFTs with larger symmetry algebras
[
20
]
. Moreover, using this relation, the conformal bootstrap method can be applied to geometrical problems. In particular, in harmonic analysis on compact cosets of
PSL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, numerical bootstrap techniques lead to bounds on eigenvalues of the Laplace operator
[
55
]
.

Coulomb gas integrals

To conclude our discussion of the use of free boson techniques in Liouville theory, let us review
the relation between certain Liouville correlation functions, and free bosonic correlation functions
[
30
]
.
The relevant Liouville correlation functions are
N
-point functions
\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle^{\text{Liouville}}
at
\sum\alpha_{i}=Q-bm
where
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
For the Liouville three-point structure constants
C
and
\hat{C}
, we have in this case

\displaystyle\lim_{\sum\alpha_{i}\to Q-bm}\hat{C}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}=\underset{\sum\alpha_{i}=Q-bm}{\operatorname{Res}}C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}\ .

(4.1.78)

This follows from Eq. (
3.1.43
) in the case
m=0
, from which the cases
m\neq 0
are obtained using the shift equations.
For
c\notin]-\infty,1]
, our Liouville
N
-point function has a simple pole at
\sum\alpha_{i}=Q-bm
, with the residue

\displaystyle\underset{\sum\alpha_{i}=Q-bm}{\operatorname{Res}}\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle^{\text{Liouville}}=\underset{\sum\alpha_{i}=Q-bm}{\operatorname{Res}}\left\langle e^{-\frac{\mu^{b}}{\pi\gamma(b^{2})}\int_{\mathbb{C}}V_{b}(z)d^{2}z}\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle^{\text{Free boson}}\ ,

(4.1.79)

where
\mu
is the cosmological constant.
On the free boson side, taking the residue of a correlation function (
4.1.53
) means stripping out a delta function, and

\underset{\sum\alpha_{i}=Q-bm}{\operatorname{Res}}\left\langle e^{-\frac{\mu^{b}}{\pi\gamma(b^{2})}\int_{\mathbb{C}}V_{b}(z)d^{2}z}\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle^{\text{Free boson}}\\
=\frac{1}{m!}\left[-\frac{\mu^{b}}{\pi\gamma(b^{2})}\right]^{m}\prod_{i<j}|z_{ij}|^{-4\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}}\int_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m}}\prod_{k=1}^{m}d^{2}y_{k}\ \prod_{k,i}|y_{k}-z_{i}|^{-4b\alpha_{i}}\prod_{k<\ell}|y_{k\ell}|^{-4b^{2}}\ .

(4.1.80)

The relation (
4.1.79
) implies that Liouville theory can be viewed as a perturbation of a free bosonic theory.
This is an example of
conformal perturbation theory
– the definition and study of two-dimensional quantum field theories (conformal or not) as perturbed conformal field theories.
The perturbing operator, here
V_{b}
, is chosen so that its conformal dimension is one, a necessary but not sufficient condition for the perturbed theory to still have conformal symmetry.
This example is rather pathological, as Liouville theory does not have a smooth
\mu\to 0
limit where we could recover a free bosonic theory, and we do not really compute Liouville theory correlation functions, but only their residues at certain poles.

The integral (
4.1.80
) is called a
Coulomb gas integral
or Dotsenko–Fateev integral. Such integrals can be used for computing correlation functions not only in Liouville theory, but also in other CFTs, including minimal models.
Since the number of integrals to be performed depends on
\alpha_{i}
and not just on
N
, Coulomb gas integrals
are less appealing for numerical computations than using decompositions into conformal blocks, together with Zamolodchikov’s recursion.
Coulomb gas integrals
coincide with partition functions of random matrix models
[
41
]
, and we have the following matrix model interpretation of certain CFT quantities:

CFT

Random matrix model

N
-point function

partition function

F,W(y)
in Eq. (
4.1.57
)

free energy, resolvent

Eq. (
4.1.62
) for
W^{(0)}(y)

loop equation

m
in Eq. (
4.1.79
)

matrix size

y_{k}
in Eq. (
4.1.80
)

matrix eigenvalue

4.2
Nonabelian affine symmetry

We will now build an affine Lie algebra
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
from a simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, in the same way as we built the affine Lie algebra
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
from the abelian Lie algebra
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
.
We will use the smallest simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
as the main example.

Affine Lie algebras are special cases of Kac–Moody algebras, which are themselves special cases of Lie algebras. For more details on these algebras, their representations, and their classification using Cartan matrices, see
[
42
]
.

4.2.1
Symmetry algebra

Reminders on Lie algebras

A Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is defined by generators
t^{a}
and commutation relations

\displaystyle[t^{a},t^{b}]=f^{ab}_{c}t^{c}\ ,

(4.2.1)

where the structure constants
f^{ab}_{c}
are numbers.
The commutation relations are assumed to obey two axioms:

antisymmetry

\displaystyle:\quad[t^{a},t^{b}]+[t^{b},t^{a}]=0\ ,

(4.2.2)

Jacobi identities

\displaystyle:\quad[t^{a},[t^{b},t^{c}]]+[t^{b},[t^{c},t^{a}]]+[t^{c},[t^{a},t^{b}]]=0\ .

(4.2.3)

For example, we already encountered the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
(
2.2.27
) in our study of global conformal symmetry.
Let us define the
Killing form

\displaystyle K^{ab}=\frac{1}{2g}\operatorname{Tr}\left(\operatorname{ad}_{t^{a}}\operatorname{ad}_{t^{b}}\right)=\frac{1}{2g}f^{ac}_{d}f^{bd}_{c}\overset{\mathfrak{sl}_{n}}{=}\operatorname{Tr}_{f}t^{a}t^{b}\ ,

(4.2.4)

where
\operatorname{ad}_{t}(t^{\prime})=[t,t^{\prime}]
is the adjoint action,
f
is the
n
-dimensional fundamental representation of
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
, and
g
is the dual Coxeter number of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, in particular
g\overset{\mathfrak{sl}_{n}}{=}n
.
The normalization factor
\frac{1}{2g}
is included so that the level, which we will shortly define in Eq. (
4.2.7
), takes integer values in rational models.
We assume that the Killing form is non-degenerate, which is equivalent to
\mathfrak{g}
being semi-simple.
Using the Killing form to raise indices, we define

\displaystyle f^{abc}=K^{ad}f_{d}^{bc}=\frac{1}{2g}\operatorname{Tr}\left(\operatorname{ad}_{t^{a}}[\operatorname{ad}_{t^{b}},\operatorname{ad}_{t^{c}}]\right)\ ,

(4.2.5)

whose second expression, which is manifestly totally antisymmetric, follows from the Jacobi identities.
The total antisymmetry of
f^{abc}
implies that the
quadratic Casimir operator

\displaystyle C_{2}=K_{ab}t^{a}t^{b}\overset{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}{=}2t^{0}t^{0}+t^{+}t^{-}+t^{-}t^{+}\ ,

(4.2.6)

is a central element of the universal enveloping algebra
U(\mathfrak{g})
, that is
[C_{2},t^{a}]=0
.

Currents and Sugawara construction

Let us build conformal field theories based on a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.
We introduce a number
\dim\mathfrak{g}
of holomorphic
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
currents

J^{a}(y)
characterized by the OPEs

\displaystyle\boxed{J^{a}(y)J^{b}(z)=\frac{kK^{ab}}{(y-z)^{2}}+\frac{f^{ab}_{c}J^{c}(z)}{y-z}+O(1)}\ ,

(4.2.7)

where the parameter
k
is called the
level
. (This parameter did not appear in the OPE (
4.1.1
) of the
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
current
J
with itself, as it could be absorbed in a rescaling of
J
.) For example, the OPEs of
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
currents are

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}J^{0}(y)J^{0}(z)=\frac{\frac{k}{2}}{(y-z)^{2}}+O(1)\ ,&J^{0}(y)J^{\pm}(z)=\frac{\pm J^{\pm}(z)}{y-z}+O(1)\ ,\\
J^{\pm}(y)J^{\pm}(z)=O(1)\ ,&J^{+}(y)J^{-}(z)=\frac{k}{(y-z)^{2}}+\frac{2J^{0}(z)}{y-z}+O(1)\ .\end{array}

Let us now introduce the
Sugawara construction
of a field
T
as a quadratic combination of
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
currents,

\displaystyle\boxed{T=\frac{K_{ab}(J^{a}J^{b})}{2(k+g)}}\ .

(4.2.10)

Then
T
is a Virasoro field with the central charge

\displaystyle\boxed{c=\frac{k\dim\mathfrak{g}}{k+g}}\ ,

(4.2.11)

such that
J^{a}
is a primary field of conformal dimension one,

\displaystyle\boxed{T(y)J^{a}(z)={\frac{\partial}{\partial z}}\frac{1}{y-z}J^{a}(z)+O(1)}\ .

(4.2.12)

(See Exercise
4.7
.)
We identify
T
with the energy-momentum tensor.
(See Exercise
4.8
for the generalized energy-momentum tensor
\hat{T}=T+Q_{a}\partial J^{a}
.)

Affine Lie algebra

We do not repeat the definition (
4.1.11
) of the modes
J^{a}_{n}
of
J^{a}
.
These modes obey the commutation relations

\displaystyle\boxed{[J^{a}_{m},J^{b}_{n}]=f^{ab}_{c}J^{c}_{m+n}+kmK^{ab}\ \delta_{m+n,0}}\ ,

(4.2.13)

which define the
affine Lie algebra

\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
.
The OPE (
4.2.12
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle[L_{m},J^{a}_{n}]=-nJ^{a}_{m+n}\ ,

(4.2.14)

and the Sugawara construction (
4.2.10
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle L_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{K_{ab}}{2(k+g)}\sum_{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}}J^{a}_{n-m}J^{b}_{m}\ ,\qquad(n\neq 0)\ ,

(4.2.15)

\displaystyle L_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{K_{ab}}{2(k+g)}\left(2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}J^{a}_{-m}J^{b}_{m}+J^{a}_{0}J^{b}_{0}\right)\ .

(4.2.16)

4.2.2
Fields, representations and fusion rules

Affine primary fields

Given a representation
\mathcal{R}
of
\mathfrak{g}
, we define an
affine primary field

\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}(z_{0})
by its OPE with
J^{a}(y)
,

\displaystyle\boxed{J^{a}(y)\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}(z_{0})=\frac{-t^{a}\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}(z_{0})}{y-z_{0}}+O(1)}\ .

(4.2.17)

This assumes that for any
z_{0}
, the field
\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}
is a vector in the representation
\mathcal{R}
. Given a basis
(e^{i})
of
\mathcal{R}
, the field
\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}=\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}_{i}e^{i}
is a collection of scalar fields
\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}_{i}
, such that
J^{a}(y)\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}_{i}(z_{0})=\frac{-(t^{a})^{j}_{i}\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}(z_{0})_{j}}{y-z_{0}}+O(1)
. This OPE involves the transpose
(t^{a})^{T}
of a Lie algebra generator. Transposition flips the sign of commutation relations, which is why we have a minus sign in Eq. (
4.2.17
). This minus sign is needed for
the associativity of the
J^{a}J^{b}\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}
OPE. (See Exercise
4.9
.)

Let us
compute the OPE
T(y)\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}(z_{0})
, by applying Wick’s theorem to
\mbox{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.0pt}{1.0pt}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 6.7639pt\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}\rule[-6.5pt]{6.7639pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{17.35423pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$(z_{0})$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{15.23349pt}{0.3pt}\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}}\mbox{$(J^{a}J_{a})$}}(y)
, using Eq. (
4.2.17
) in the form
\mbox{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.0pt}{1.0pt}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 6.7639pt\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}\rule[-6.5pt]{6.7639pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{17.35423pt}{0.3pt}}\mbox{$(z_{0})$}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule[-6.5pt]{5.67229pt}{0.3pt}\makebox[0.0pt]{\rule[-6.5pt]{0.3pt}{3.0pt}}}\mbox{$J^{a}$}}(x)=\frac{-t^{a}\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}(x)}{x-z_{0}}
.
We find

\displaystyle T(y)\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}(z_{0})=\frac{K_{ab}t^{a}}{k+g}\left(\frac{\frac{1}{2}t^{b}\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}(z_{0})}{(y-z_{0})^{2}}-\frac{(J^{b}\Phi^{\mathcal{R}})(z_{0})}{y-z_{0}}\right)+O(1)\ .

(4.2.18)

Because
T
is the energy-momentum tensor and obeys Axiom
2.2
, this determines the derivative of
\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}
in terms of the action of currents,

\displaystyle\partial\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}=-\frac{K_{ab}t^{a}(J^{b}\Phi^{\mathcal{R}})}{k+g}\ .

(4.2.19)

Then, we see that our
T\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}
OPE is
of the form (
2.2.9
), which shows that
\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}
is a primary field with the conformal dimension

\displaystyle\boxed{\Delta_{\mathcal{R}}=\frac{C_{2}(\mathcal{R})}{2(k+g)}}\ ,

(4.2.20)

where
C_{2}(\mathcal{R})
is the eigenvalue of the quadratic Casimir operator
C_{2}
(
4.2.6
) when acting in the representation
\mathcal{R}
. (We are assuming that
\mathcal{R}
is indecomposable, so that it contains only one eigenspace of
C_{2}
.)

Let us now consider the states
|v^{\mathcal{R}}\rangle
that correspond to the
\mathcal{R}
-valued field
\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}(z_{0})
by the state-field correspondence.
The definition (
4.2.17
) of
\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}(z_{0})
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}J^{a}_{n>0}|v^{\mathcal{R}}\rangle=0\ ,\\
J^{a}_{0}|v^{\mathcal{R}}\rangle=-t^{a}|v^{\mathcal{R}}\rangle\ .\end{array}\right.

The states
|v^{\mathcal{R}}\rangle
are killed by the annihilation operators, and are called
affine primary states
.
They transform in the representation
\mathcal{R}
of the
horizontal subalgebra

\mathfrak{g}\subset\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
generated by
\{J^{a}_{0}\}
.
Acting on the affine primary states with creation operators
J^{a}_{n<0}
generates affine descendant states, which form an
affine highest-weight representation

\hat{\mathcal{R}}
of the affine Lie algebra
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Isospin variables

Rather than introducing a discrete basis of the representation
\mathcal{R}
, it is often convenient to represent states in
\mathcal{R}

as functions of an
isospin variable

X
.
Affine primary fields become functions
\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}_{X}(z_{0})
of
X
, on which
t^{a}
acts as a differential operator
D_{X}^{\mathcal{R}}(t^{a})
,
and we have

\displaystyle J^{a}(y)\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}_{X}(z_{0})=\frac{-D^{\mathcal{R}}_{X}(t^{a})\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}_{X}(z_{0})}{y-z_{0}}+O(1)\quad\text{with}\quad t^{a}\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}_{X}(z_{0})=D_{X}^{\mathcal{R}}(t^{a})\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}_{X}(z_{0})\ .

(4.2.23)

The differential operators
D_{X}^{\mathcal{R}}(t^{a})
obey the commutation relations of
\mathfrak{g}
, and their quadratic invariant combination is
C_{2}(\mathcal{R})
,

\displaystyle[D_{X}^{\mathcal{R}}(t^{a}),D_{X}^{\mathcal{R}}(t^{b})]=f^{ab}_{c}D_{X}^{\mathcal{R}}(t^{c})\ ,\qquad K_{ab}D_{X}^{\mathcal{R}}(t^{a})D_{X}^{\mathcal{R}}(t^{b})=C_{2}(\mathcal{R})\ .

(4.2.24)

The isospin variable
X
is a collection of as many complex variables as
\mathfrak{g}
has positive roots.
In particular, an
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
isospin variable has
\frac{n(n-1)}{2}
components, and an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
isospin variable has one component.
We already know differential operators
D^{j}_{x}(t^{a})
(
2.2.3
) that
obey the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
commutation relations (
2.2.27
) for any choice of the

spin

j
.
The eigenvalue of the quadratic Casimir operator in the corresponding representation of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
is

\displaystyle C_{2}(j)=K_{ab}D_{x}^{j}(t^{a})D_{x}^{j}(t^{b})=2j(j+1)\ ,

(4.2.25)

and the conformal dimension of the corresponding field is

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}=\frac{j(j+1)}{k+2}\ .

(4.2.26)

Another triple of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
differential operators is given by

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}D_{\mu}^{j}(t^{-})=-\mu\ ,\\
D_{\mu}^{j}(t^{0})=-\mu{\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}}\ ,\\
D_{\mu}^{j}(t^{+})=\mu\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\mu^{2}}-\frac{j(j+1)}{\mu}\ .\end{array}\right.

The
x
-basis field

\Phi^{j}_{x}(z_{0})
and
\mu
-basis field

\Phi^{j}_{\mu}(z_{0})
are related by the formal Fourier transform

\displaystyle\Phi_{x}^{j}(z_{0})=\int d\mu\ \mu^{-j-1}e^{\mu x}\Phi_{\mu}^{j}(z_{0})\ .

(4.2.30)

The
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
currents and
\mu
-basis fields can be represented in terms of free fields.
This
Wakimoto free-field representation
of
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
is described in Exercise
4.10
.

OPEs and fusion rules

Let us study the fusion rules of affine highest-weight representations, equivalently the OPEs of affine primary fields.
To write such OPEs, we will omit the dependences on the positions
z_{i}
of the fields, which are dictated by conformal symmetry since affine primary fields are also primary fields.
We will also omit the affine descendant fields because, as in the cases of the Virasoro and
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
algebras, the contributions of affine descendant fields are determined by the contributions of the affine primary fields. (This would however not be true in the case of a
W
-algebra.) So we write a generic OPE as

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mathcal{R}_{1}}_{X_{1}}\Phi^{\mathcal{R}_{2}}_{X_{2}}\sim\sum_{\mathcal{R}_{3}}\int dX_{3}\ C^{\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}}_{\mathcal{R}_{3}}(X_{1},X_{2}|X_{3})\Phi^{\mathcal{R}_{3}}_{X_{3}}\ .

(4.2.31)

Inserting
\oint dz\,J^{a}(z)
on both sides, and using the linear independence of the operators
\Phi^{\mathcal{R}_{3}}_{X_{3}}
, we obtain an equation for the structure function
C^{\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}}_{\mathcal{R}_{3}}(X_{1},X_{2}|X_{3})
,

\displaystyle\left(D_{X_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}_{1}}(t^{a})+D_{X_{2}}^{\mathcal{R}_{2}}(t^{a})-\left(D_{X_{3}}^{\mathcal{R}_{3}}(t^{a})\right)^{\dagger}\right)C^{\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}}_{\mathcal{R}_{3}}(X_{1},X_{2}|X_{3})=0\ ,

(4.2.32)

where the dagger denotes the Hermitian conjugate for
X_{3}
-differential operators, such that for any functions
f,g
we have

\int fDg=\int gD^{\dagger}f
.
This equation characterizes
C^{\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}}_{\mathcal{R}_{3}}(X_{1},X_{2}|X_{3})
as an intertwiner between the representations
\mathcal{R}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{R}_{2}
and
\mathcal{R}_{3}
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.
This shows that the fusion multiplicities of affine highest-weight representations of
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
are bounded by the tensor product multiplicities of the underlying representations of
\mathfrak{g}
,

\displaystyle m_{\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{1},\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{2}}^{\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{3}}\leq m_{\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}}^{\mathcal{R}_{3}}\ .

(4.2.33)

The presence of null vectors in the representations
\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{i}
can lead to extra conditions on the structure function
C^{\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}}_{\mathcal{R}_{3}}(X_{1},X_{2}|X_{3})
, in which case
m_{\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{1},\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{2}}^{\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{3}}<m_{\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}}^{\mathcal{R}_{3}}
.
And nothing guarantees that only highest-weight representations appear in the fusion product of two highest-weight representations.
In the case of the
\widetilde{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
WZW model, other types of representations do appear. (See Section
4.4.3
.)

For generic representations
\mathcal{R}_{i}
, the multiplicity
m_{\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}}^{\mathcal{R}_{3}}
is the number of linearly independent solutions of Eq. (
4.2.32
) in the absence of further constraints, namely

\displaystyle m_{\mathrm{max}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}2\quad\text{if}\ \mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\ ,\\
\infty\quad\text{if}\ \mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{n\geq 3}\ .\end{array}\right.

We indeed have a set of
\dim\mathfrak{sl}_{n}=n^{2}-1
equations, for a function
C^{\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}}_{\mathcal{R}_{3}}(X_{1},X_{2}|X_{3})
of
3\frac{n(n-1)}{2}
variables – the components of
X_{1},X_{2}
and
X_{3}
.
If
n=2
there are three equations and three variables, and this can actually be reduced to one second-order differential equation for a function of one variable. (See Eq. (
4.2.43
).)
If
n\geq 3
there are more variables than equations, so that
C^{\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}}_{\mathcal{R}_{3}}(X_{1},X_{2}|X_{3})
has an arbitrary dependence on a number of variables.
In the case of finite-dimensional representations of
\mathfrak{sl}_{n\geq 3}
, the multiplicities are of course finite, but they can take arbitrarily high values, depending on the involved representations.

4.2.3
Ward identities and Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov equations

Ward identities

The Sugawara construction of the energy-momentum tensor
T(y)
from the currents
J^{a}(y)
, and the behaviour (
2.2.10
) of
T(y)
near
y=\infty
, suggest

\displaystyle\boxed{J^{a}(y)\underset{y\to\infty}{=}O\left(\frac{1}{y^{2}}\right)}\ .

(4.2.36)

Given
N
fields
\Phi^{\sigma_{i}}(z_{i})
, and a meromorphic function
\epsilon(z)
, with no poles outside
\{z_{1},\cdots z_{N}\}
, we therefore have

\displaystyle\oint_{\infty}dy\ \epsilon(y)\left\langle J^{a}(y)\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{\sigma_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=0\quad\text{provided}\quad\epsilon(y)\underset{y\to\infty}{=}O(1)\ ,

(4.2.37)

In the case
\epsilon(y)=\frac{1}{(y-z_{i})^{n}}
with
n\geq 1
, we obtain the
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
local Ward identities, which are formally identical to the
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
local Ward identities (
4.1.28
).
In particular, if all fields are affine primaries except possibly the field at
z_{i}
, we find

\displaystyle\left\langle J^{a}_{-n}\Phi^{\sigma_{i}}(z_{i})\prod_{j\neq i}\Phi^{\mathcal{R}_{j}}_{X_{j}}(z_{j})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{D_{X_{j}}^{\mathcal{R}_{j}}(t^{a})}{(z_{j}-z_{i})^{n}}\left\langle\Phi^{\sigma_{i}}(z_{i})\prod_{j\neq i}\Phi^{\mathcal{R}_{j}}_{X_{j}}(z_{j})\right\rangle\ .

(4.2.38)

In the case
\epsilon(y)=1
, we obtain the
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
global Ward identities,

\displaystyle\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{N}(J_{0}^{a})^{(z_{i})}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{\sigma_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=0\ .

(4.2.39)

Let us specialize to correlation functions involving only affine primary fields.
Knowing the poles (
4.2.17
) of
J^{a}(y)
and its behaviour near
y=\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle J^{a}(y)\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{\mathcal{R}_{i}}_{X_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{D^{\mathcal{R}_{i}}_{X_{i}}(t^{a})}{y-z_{i}}\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{\mathcal{R}_{i}}_{X_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle\ ,

(4.2.40)

and the global Ward identities become

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}D_{X_{i}}^{\mathcal{R}_{i}}(t^{a})\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{\mathcal{R}_{i}}_{X_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=0\ .

(4.2.41)

\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
global Ward identities

In the
x
-basis, the
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
global Ward identities are formally identical to the Virasoro global Ward identities (
2.2.23
). In the case
N=3
, the solution is Eq. (
2.2.42
), so that

\displaystyle\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{3}\Phi^{j_{i}}_{x_{i}}\right\rangle\propto\ x_{12}^{j_{1}+j_{2}-j_{3}}x_{23}^{j_{2}+j_{3}-j_{1}}x_{31}^{j_{3}+j_{1}-j_{2}}\ ,

(4.2.42)

where we omitted the dependence on
z_{i}
.
In the
\mu
-basis, we find

\displaystyle\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{3}\Phi^{j_{i}}_{\mu_{i}}\right\rangle\propto\ \mu_{2}\delta(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\mu_{3})\mathcal{H}\left(-\frac{\mu_{1}}{\mu_{2}}\right)\ ,

(4.2.43)

where the function
\mathcal{H}(x)
, which parametrizes the general solution of the
t^{-}
and
t^{0}
equations, is constrained by the
t^{+}
equation to obey the twisted hypergeometric differential equation (
2.3.41
).
It may seem strange that in the
\mu
-basis we obtain a second-order differential equation, whereas in the
x
-basis the space of solutions appeared to be one-dimensional.
The number of linearly independent solutions of the global Ward identities has the algebraic interpretation of a tensor product multiplicity for
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
representations, and this should not depend on our choice of isospin variable.
Actually it is the
x
-basis calculation that is misleading: analyticity conditions on the
x_{i}
-dependence of
\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{3}\Phi^{j_{i}}_{x_{i}}\right\rangle
in general allow the existence of two solutions that differ globally, although they are locally identical
[
43
]
.
The tensor product multiplicity for generic
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
representations is two, as correctly suggested by the
\mu
-basis calculation.

Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov equations

Inserting the equation (
4.2.19
) in an
N
-point function of affine primary fields, and applying the local Ward identity (
4.2.38
) to
J^{b}_{-1}\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}_{X}=(J^{b}\Phi^{\mathcal{R}}_{X})
, we obtain the Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov equations or
KZ equations
,

\displaystyle\boxed{\left\{(k+g){\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}}+\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{K_{ab}D_{X_{i}}^{\mathcal{R}_{i}}(t^{a})D_{X_{j}}^{\mathcal{R}_{j}}(t^{b})}{z_{i}-z_{j}}\right\}\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{\mathcal{R}_{i}}_{X_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=0}\ .

(4.2.44)

These are first-order differential equations in
z_{i}
, and like the analogous equations (
4.1.37
) for free boson correlation functions, they determine the dependence on
z_{i}
of correlation functions of primary fields.
However, unlike the free boson equations, the KZ equations do not have simple solutions in general.
It can be checked that the KZ equations imply the conformal global Ward identities. (See Exercise
4.11
.)

The KZ equations can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\left\{(k+g){\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}}+H_{i}\right\}\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{\mathcal{R}_{i}}_{X_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=0\ ,

(4.2.45)

where
H_{i}
are the mutually commuting Gaudin Hamiltonians, the Hamiltonians of the
Gaudin model
– an integrable model associated with the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.
Techniques developed for studying the Gaudin model can be useful for solving the KZ equations, as we will now see in the case
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
.

4.2.4
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
case: the KZ-BPZ relation

We will now show that the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
KZ equations are equivalent to certain BPZ equations via Sklyanin’s separation of variables for the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
Gaudin model.

Reformulation of the KZ equations

Our derivation of the KZ equations amounted to inserting the Sugawara construction (
4.2.10
) at the points
z_{i}
in the
N
-point function
\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{j_{i}}_{X_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle
of affine primary fields.
Inserting the Sugawara construction at an arbitrary point
y
instead, we obtain the identity

\displaystyle\left\langle\left(T(y)-\frac{1}{2(k+2)}\left[2(J^{0}J^{0})(y)+(J^{+}J^{-})(y)+(J^{-}J^{+})(y)\right]\right)\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{j_{i}}_{X_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=0\ .

(4.2.46)

According to Eqs. (
2.2.17
) and (
4.2.40
), inserting the fields
T(y)
and
J^{a}(y)
amounts to acting with the differential operators

\displaystyle\hat{T}(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{\Delta_{j_{i}}}{(z-z_{i})^{2}}+\frac{1}{z-z_{i}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}}\right)\ ,

(4.2.47)

\displaystyle\hat{J}^{a}(y)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{D^{\mathcal{R}_{i}}_{X_{i}}(t^{a})}{y-z_{i}}\ ,

(4.2.48)

where
\Delta_{j}
is defined in Eq. (
4.2.26
).
These differential operators obey the commutation relations

\displaystyle\left[\hat{T}(y),\hat{J}^{a}(z)\right]

\displaystyle={\frac{\partial}{\partial z}}\frac{\hat{J}^{a}(y)-\hat{J}^{a}(z)}{y-z}\ ,

(4.2.49)

\displaystyle\left[\hat{J}^{a}(y),\hat{J}^{b}(z)\right]

\displaystyle=f^{ab}_{c}\frac{\hat{J}^{c}(y)-\hat{J}^{c}(z)}{y-z}\ ,

(4.2.50)

where
f^{ab}_{c}
are the structure constants of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
, as encoded in the commutation relations (
2.2.27
).
And one can easily show that inserting a normal-ordered product, for instance
(J^{-}J^{+})(y)
, amounts to acting with the product of the corresponding differential operators.
Therefore, the identity (
4.2.46
) amounts to the differential equation

\displaystyle\left(\hat{T}(y)-\frac{1}{2(k+2)}\left[2\hat{J}^{0}(y)\hat{J}^{0}(y)+\hat{J}^{+}(y)\hat{J}^{-}(y)+\hat{J}^{-}(y)\hat{J}^{+}(y)\right]\right)\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{j_{i}}_{X_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=0\ .

(4.2.51)

Insertion of the zeros of
\hat{J}^{-}(y)

Let us simplify Eq. (
4.2.51
) by taking
y
to be one of the zeros
\hat{y}_{j}
of
\hat{J}^{-}(y)
.
These zeros are differential operators, and the value of a
y
-dependent differential operator
\hat{\mathcal{O}}(y)
at
y=\hat{y}_{j}
is defined by inserting
\hat{y}_{j}
from the left, that is
\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\hat{y}_{j})=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\hat{y}_{j}}\frac{dy}{y-\hat{y}_{j}}\hat{\mathcal{O}}(y)
.
Using Eq. (
4.2.50
) for bringing the
\hat{J}^{-}(y)
factors to the left, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(\hat{T}(\hat{y}_{j})-\frac{1}{k+2}\left[(\hat{J}^{0})^{2}(\hat{y}_{j})+\partial\hat{J}^{0}(\hat{y}_{j})\right]\right)\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{j_{i}}_{X_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=0\ .

(4.2.52)

Let us further study the differential operators
\hat{y}_{j}
and
\hat{J}^{0}(\hat{y}_{j})
.
According to Eq. (
4.2.50
), we have
[\hat{J}^{-}(y),\hat{J}^{-}(z)]=0
.
Therefore
[\hat{y}_{j},\hat{y}_{k}]=0
, and

\displaystyle\boxed{\hat{J}^{-}(y)=\hat{Y}_{2}\frac{\prod_{j}(y-\hat{y}_{j})}{\prod_{i}(y-z_{i})}}\ ,

(4.2.53)

where
\hat{Y}_{2}
is a differential operator such that
[\hat{Y}_{2},\hat{y}_{j}]=0
.
Using Eq. (
4.2.50
) we find
[\hat{J}^{0}(\hat{y}_{j}),\hat{J}^{-}(z)]=\frac{\hat{J}^{-}(z)}{\hat{y}_{j}-z}
, and deduce

\displaystyle[\hat{p}_{j},\hat{Y}_{2}]=0\quad\text{and}\quad[\hat{p}_{j},\hat{y}_{k}]=\delta_{j,k}\quad\text{where}\quad\hat{p}_{j}=\hat{J}^{0}(\hat{y}_{j})\ .

(4.2.54)

We can now simplify the second term of Eq. (
4.2.52
),

(\hat{J}^{0})^{2}(\hat{y}_{j})+\partial\hat{J}^{0}(\hat{y}_{j})=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\hat{y}_{j}}\frac{dy}{y-\hat{y}_{j}}\left(\hat{J}^{0}(y)+{\frac{\partial}{\partial y}}\right)\hat{J}^{0}(y)\\
=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\hat{y}_{j}}\frac{dy}{y-\hat{y}_{j}}\left(\hat{p}_{j}+{\frac{\partial}{\partial y}}\right)\hat{J}^{0}(y)=\hat{p}_{j}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\hat{y}_{j}}\frac{dy}{y-\hat{y}_{j}}\hat{J}^{0}(y)=\hat{p}_{j}^{2}\ .

(4.2.55)

So Eq. (
4.2.52
) becomes

\displaystyle\boxed{\left(\frac{1}{k+2}\hat{p}_{j}^{2}-\hat{T}(\hat{y}_{j})\right)\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{j_{i}}_{X_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=0}\ .

(4.2.56)

Sklyanin’s separation of variables

Let us define the Sklyanin variable
y_{j}
as the eigenvalues of the mutually commuting operators
\hat{y}_{j}
.
The
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
global Ward identity (
4.2.41
) associated with the current
J^{a}
can be written as

\displaystyle\underset{y\to\infty}{\lim}y\hat{J}^{a}(y)\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{j_{i}}_{X_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=0\ .

(4.2.57)

When combined with the definition (
4.2.53
) of
\hat{y}_{j}
, the Ward identity associated with
J^{-}
suggests that we have
N-2
Sklyanin variables
y_{j}
.
Let us define two more variables as the eigenvalues
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
of the operators
\hat{Y}_{1}
and
\hat{Y}_{2}
(
4.2.53
), where we define

\displaystyle\hat{Y}_{1}=\underset{y\to\infty}{\lim}y\hat{J}^{-}(y)\ .

(4.2.58)

We can now define
Sklyanin’s separation of variables
for the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
Gaudin model as the linear
map
\mathcal{K}
from functions of
(X_{1},\cdots X_{N})
to functions of
(Y_{1},Y_{2},y_{1},\cdots y_{N-2})
that diagonalizes the operators
(\hat{Y}_{1},\hat{Y}_{2},\hat{y}_{1},\cdots\hat{y}_{N-2})
, so that in particular
\mathcal{K}\hat{y}_{j}f(X_{1},\cdots X_{N})=y_{j}\mathcal{K}f(X_{1},\cdots X_{N})
.
We actually only define this map on
functions that obey the global Ward identity associated with
J^{-}
i.e.
that are killed by
\hat{Y}_{1}
, whose images therefore have a
\delta(Y_{1})
prefactor.
Using in addition the global Ward identity associated with
J^{0}
, we find that the combined dependence of
\mathcal{K}\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{j_{i}}_{X_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle
on
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
is a
\delta(Y_{1})Y_{2}
prefactor.
(This is done with the help of Eq. (
4.2.50
), which implies
\left[\underset{y\to\infty}{\lim}y\hat{J}^{0}(y),\hat{J}^{-}(z)\right]=-\hat{J}^{-}(z)
.)

Let us rewrite our equation (
4.2.56
) in terms of Sklyanin variables.
Using Eq. (
4.2.54
), we find

\mathcal{K}\hat{p}_{j}\mathcal{K}^{-1}={\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{j}}}
.
Therefore,
Eq. (
4.2.56
) is equivalent to the following equation for
\mathcal{K}\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{j_{i}}_{X_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle
,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{k+2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y_{j}^{2}}-\mathcal{K}\hat{T}(\hat{y}_{j})\mathcal{K}^{-1}\right)\mathcal{K}\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{j_{i}}_{X_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=0\ .

(4.2.59)

This equation apparently only involves
y_{j}
for a given index
j
, whereas each KZ equation involves
(X_{1},\cdots X_{N})
.
So it may seem that using Sklyanin variables leads to a separation of variables not only in the Gaudin model, but also
in the KZ equations.
This is however not true, because the differential operator
\hat{T}(y)
(
4.2.47
) involves
z_{i}
-derivatives at fixed isospin variables
X_{i}
.
When writing
\mathcal{K}\hat{T}(\hat{y}_{j})\mathcal{K}^{-1}
, we have to use
z_{i}
-derivatives at fixed Sklyanin variables
y_{k}
, and we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\hat{T}(\hat{y}_{j})\mathcal{K}^{-1}=\sum_{i}\left[\frac{\Delta_{j_{i}}}{(y_{j}-z_{i})^{2}}+\frac{1}{y_{j}-z_{i}}\left({\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}}+{\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{j}}}\right)\right]-\sum_{k\neq j}\frac{1}{y_{jk}}\left({\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{j}}}-{\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{k}}}\right)\ .

(4.2.60)

(See Exercise
4.12
.)
Introducing the function

\displaystyle\Theta_{N}=\frac{\prod_{i<i^{\prime}\leq N}(z_{i}-z_{i^{\prime}})\prod_{j<j^{\prime}\leq N-2}(y_{j}-y_{j^{\prime}})}{\prod_{i=1}^{N}\prod_{j=1}^{N-2}(z_{i}-y_{j})}\ ,

(4.2.61)

the equation (
4.2.59
) is equivalent to

\left\{\frac{1}{k+2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y_{j}^{2}}-\sum_{i}\frac{1}{y_{j}-z_{i}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}}-\sum_{k\neq j}\frac{1}{y_{j}-y_{k}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{k}}}\right.\\
\left.-\sum_{i}\frac{\Delta_{j_{i}}-\frac{k}{4}}{(y_{j}-z_{i})^{2}}-\sum_{k\neq j}\frac{\frac{3k}{4}+1}{(y_{j}-y_{k})^{2}}\right\}\Theta_{N}^{\frac{k+2}{2}}\mathcal{K}\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{j_{i}}_{X_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle=0\ .

(4.2.62)

KZ-BPZ relation for conformal blocks

As an equation for
\Theta_{N}^{\frac{k+2}{2}}\mathcal{K}\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{j_{i}}_{X_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle
,
Eq. (
4.2.62
) coincides with the BPZ equation (
2.3.24
) for the correlation function of
\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{P_{i}}(z_{i})\prod_{j=1}^{N-2}V_{\langle 2,1\rangle}(y_{j})\right\rangle
, provided the parameter
b
of the Virasoro algebra is given by

\displaystyle\boxed{b^{2}=-k-2}\ ,

(4.2.63)

and the momentums
P_{i}
are given in terms of the spins
j_{i}
by

\displaystyle\boxed{P=b^{-1}\left(j+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)}\quad\implies\quad\boxed{\Delta(P)=\Delta_{j}-\frac{k}{4}}\ ,

(4.2.64)

where
\Delta(P)
is given by Eq. (
2.1.27
).
The BPZ equations only constrain the dependence on
y_{j}
, but we have already determined the dependence of

\mathcal{K}\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{j_{i}}_{X_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle
on the variables
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
.
This leads to Feigin, Frenkel and Stoyanovsky’s
KZ-BPZ relation
,

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{K}\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{j_{i}}_{X_{i}}(z_{i})\right\rangle\sim\delta(Y_{1})Y_{2}\Theta_{N}^{\frac{1}{2}b^{2}}\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{N}V_{P_{i}}(z_{i})\prod_{j=1}^{N-2}V_{\langle 2,1\rangle}(y_{j})\right\rangle}\ .

(4.2.65)

This equation means that the differential equations obeyed by both sides coincide.
By definition,
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
conformal blocks
are elements of bases of solutions of the KZ equations: we can therefore define
N
-point
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
conformal blocks from Virasoro
(2N-2)
-point conformal blocks.
In the diagrammatic representation of conformal blocks as trees, the relevant Virasoro block is obtained from an
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
conformal block by adding a degenerate field near each node, and identifying the two fusion channels of that degenerate field with
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
fusion multiplicities.
For example, we can build a basis of
s
-channel four-point
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
conformal blocks, parametrized by an
s
-channel spin
j_{s}
and two fusion multiplicities
\epsilon\in\{+,-\}
, as follows:

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{58.40889pt}{50.2643pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{91.85281pt}{0.74765pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 -9.71 C 11.81 -13.45 14.89 -23.62 23.62 -23.62}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{1.0}{-1.0}{0.0}{8.5359pt}{-7.01425pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.98 0 C 3.51 0.28 1.11 1.11 -0.55 2.08 L -0.55 -2.08 C 1.11 -1.11 3.51 -0.28 4.98 0}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
